Semisimplifying categorical Heisenberg actions and periodic equivalences
Chris Hone, Finn Klein, Bregje Pauwels, Alexander Sherman, Oded Yacobi, Victor L. Zhang

Abstract
. We systematically apply semisimplification functors in modular representation theory. Motivated by the Duflo–Serganova functor in Lie superalgebras, we construct various functors of interest. In the setting of finite groups, we refine the cyclic group Brauer construction and categorify the Glauberman correspondence. In the setting of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions, we obtain a rich collection of functors which commute with the categorical action. Applied to well-known categorifications of the basic representation and Fock space, our functors give explicit realizations of periodic equivalences for polynomial functors and symmetric groups first studied by Henke–Koenig. In fact, we globalize the equivalences of Henke–Koenig by symmetric monoidal functors. We apply these results to deduce branching properties of certain modular representations of S n S_{n} .



1.
Introduction

Representation theory in characteristic
p>0
is incredibly rich and complicated. For a finite group
G
, representations need not be semisimple, and many approaches have been developed to study the homological properties of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
. Local representation theory and support theory have been particularly successful and continue to attract widespread interest (
[
1
]
,
[
19
]
). In a separate vein, computing the decomposition numbers (or even the dimensions of irreducible representations) of the symmetric group remains one of the largest open problems in modular representation theory (
[
33
]
,
[
40
]
), and has important connections to the modular representation theory of the algebraic group
GL_{n}
.

In this paper we introduce
One Tree Island (OTI) functors
for studying
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
and categorical Heisenberg actions in characteristic
p
. Such functors were already introduced and studied in
[
2
]
in the setting of algebraic groups, where a (conjectural) connection was found with the Finkelberg–Mirkovic (FM) conjecture. The themes of
[
2
]
and this paper are that OTI functors:

(1)

have explicit, computable definitions,

(2)

are compatible with categorical structures (e.g. they are symmetric monoidal and/or define morphisms of categorical actions),

(3)

are non-exact but define maps on Grothendieck groups mod
p
,

(4)

naturally detect the cohomological support of a module,

(5)

behave well on generating objects such as permutation modules and tilting modules,

(6)

categorify a central element of the enveloping algebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{p}
modulo
p
,

(7)

and provide extensions of known functors of interest (hypercohomology composed with FM equivalence in the setting of
[
2
]
, and periodic equivalences of symmetric group representations in the setting of this paper).

Using OTI functors we obtain explicit consequences for representations of symmetric groups, which we believe further motivate the study of OTI functors in modular representation theory and categorical Heisenberg actions.

The remainder of the introduction will provide a brief description of the OTI functor and the main results of this paper. We will also discuss how this work is motivated by the Duflo–Serganova functor from the theory of Lie superalgebras.

1.1.
What is an OTI functor? The case of finite groups

Let
G
be a finite group such that
p\operatorname{\big|}|G|
. Let
\Bbbk
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p
, and let
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
be the category of finite dimensional representations of
G
over
\Bbbk
.

By Cauchy’s theorem, there exists a cyclic subgroup
H\subseteq G
with
H\cong C_{p}
. One may then consider the functor
\Phi_{H}
defined by the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Res}_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{H}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ss}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}

Here,
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is a semisimple, symmetric tensor category defined as the
semisimplification
of the tensor category
\operatorname{Rep}C_{p}
. As an abelian category, this category is just
\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
.
The semisimplication functor
ss
is symmetric monoidal, but not exact, and in this case admits an explicit, computable description given in Section
3.2
.

Thus the functor
\Phi_{H}
is defined by restricting, then semisimplifying. The centralizer subgroup
C_{G}(H)
naturally acts on
\Phi_{H}(M)
for any module
M
, and
H
acts trivially. Hence we obtain a functor

\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}C_{G}(H)/H,

where
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}C_{G}(H)/H
denotes the representations of
C_{G}(H)/H
in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
. The functor
\Phi_{H}
is our first example of an OTI functor, and it satisfies many desirable properties collected in Lemma
4.2.4
.

As an application in the finite groups setting, we consider the situation where we have a group
H
of order
p
acting on
G
. The Glauberman correspondence is a canonical bijection between the irreducible representations of
G
fixed by
H
and the irreducible representations of the invariants
G^{H}
(Theorem
4.5.1
). By Clifford theory, an irreducible representation of
G
that is fixed by
H
extends uniquely to an irreducible representation of
G\rtimes H
.
Observing that
C_{G\rtimes H}(H)/H\cong G^{H}
, we obtain an OTI functor:

\displaystyle\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G\rtimes H)\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(G^{H}).

Theorem
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{H}
above categorifies the Glauberman correspondence.

We refer to Theorem
4.5.3
for a precise statement and proof. Note that one novel aspect of this categorification is that it explains the sign that appears in the Glauberman correspondence, see
4.5
for more details.

1.2.
Periodic equivalences for symmetric groups

It was first observed in
[
27
]
(see also
[
39
]
, and
[
24
]
for a more recent treatment) that there is an equivalence between certain full abelian subcategories of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
and
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}
, corresponding to truncating the first row of a partition by
p^{r}
.

For the purposes of this introduction, we say a partition
\lambda
of
n
is
p^{r}
-stable if
\lambda_{1}\gg p^{r}
and
\sum\limits_{i>1}\lambda_{i}<p^{r}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}
be the smallest abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
containing all permutation modules
M^{\lambda}
, where
\lambda
is
p^{r}
-stable. The category
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}
contains all Specht modules for
p^{r}
-stable partitions and all simple modules for
p^{r}
-stable,
p
-regular partitions. Then
[
24
,
27
]
produce equivalences
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}
, which on simple, Specht and permutation modules correspond to taking the partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots)
to
(\lambda_{1}-p^{r},\lambda_{2},\dots)
. We call these “periodic equivalences”.

The equivalences of
[
24
,
27
]
have useful applications in computing decomposition numbers and constructing Deligne interpolation categories, but they are inexplicit and difficult to compute directly. Both functors are only defined on subcategories of
\operatorname{Rep}S_{n}
, and require passage through Schur–Weyl duality amongst other operations to define them.

Our first theorem improves upon
[
24
,
27
]
by providing explicit, global functors
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}
which restrict to the periodic equivalences.
Furthermore, our functors commute with the well-known categorical action of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}
on
\bigoplus_{n}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
.

To state our theorem, let
S_{p^{r}}\subseteq S_{n}
denote the natural subgroup determined by the permutations of
\{n-p^{r}+1,\dots,n\}
. Then
S_{p^{r}}
contains a transitive, elementary abelian subgroup
A
of order
p^{r}
. Write
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r}
for generators of
A
. Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\in\Bbbk
be linearly independent over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and set

z_{\mathbf{a}}=a_{1}(\sigma_{1}-1)+\dots+a_{r}(\sigma_{r}-1)\in\Bbbk A.

Note that
z_{\mathbf{a}}
defines an operator on any
V\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
,
which commutes with
S_{n-p^{r}}
. Hence
\operatorname{ker}(z_{\mathbf{a}}),\operatorname{im}(z_{\mathbf{a}})
, etc. can be viewed as functors from
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}
.
The following is a direct corollary of Theorem
8.3.3
.

Theorem A
.

The functors
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}
given by

\frac{\operatorname{ker}(z_{\mathbf{a}})}{\operatorname{ker}(z_{\mathbf{a}})\cap\operatorname{im}(z_{\mathbf{a}})}\ \text{ and }\ \frac{\operatorname{ker}(z_{\mathbf{a}})}{\operatorname{im}(z_{\mathbf{a}}^{p-1})}

have the following properties:

(1)

they restrict to the periodic equivalences
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}
, and

(2)

they commute with the
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}
categorical action.

Theorem
A
provides many distinct functors which globalize our equivalence of interest: we can choose any generic tuple
\mathbf{a}
and either functor given in the statement.
In fact, Theorem
8.3.3
exhibits many more global functors that satisfy (1) and (2), some of which are symmetric monoidal.
In the next section we will generalise the construction of OTI functors, and the functors above will be specific examples of this general construction.

1.3.
An OTI functor from categorical actions

Let us distill the definition of the OTI functor from the finite group case as follows. We may replace
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
and
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
by (nice) abelian categories
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}
, and replace
\operatorname{Res}_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
by a functor
R:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
with an action of
C_{p}
. Then we obtain a functor

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ss}
\textstyle{\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}.}

Here,
\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}
is the category of objects in
\mathscr{D}
with a
C_{p}
-action, and
\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is equivalent to
p-1
copies of
\mathscr{D}
. Note that the Frobenius functor is of the above form, where
R(M)=M^{\otimes p}
.

Our primary case of interest comes from the categorical action of the Heisenberg algebra.
Recall that a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathscr{C}
is the data of a pair of biadjoint functors
E,F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
and natural transformations
x:E\to E
and
T:E^{2}\to E^{2}
satisfying certain compatibilities. One such requirement is that
T
generates an action of
S_{n}
on
E^{n}
, which in particular provides an action of
C_{p}
on
E^{p}
. This provides the functor
R=E^{p}
above.

We also generalise the semisimplification functor
ss:\operatorname{Rep}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
to any
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}\to\mathscr{A}
such that
\varphi\circ\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{S_{p^{r}}}=0
for all non-transitive subgroups
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
, see Section
5.1
.
We call such a functor
\varphi
a
CF functor
, a modest generalisation of the notion of
\mathbf{V}
-functor introduced in
[
10
]
. CF functors satisfy the minimal conditions necessary for our machinery to behave well.

Now given a category
\mathscr{C}
with a degenerate Heisenberg action and a CF functor
\varphi
, we define the OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
by the following (now familiar) diagram:

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E^{p^{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\boxtimes\varphi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}}

There are many interesting, well-studied examples of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions,
the most famous being on the category
\mathcal{S}ym:=\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
. In this case,
\Phi_{\varphi}
will be symmetric monoidal whenever
\varphi
is. For example, if
p^{r}=p
and
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is given by restriction to
C_{p}
and semisimplification, we obtain an OTI functor
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
(see Example
4.2.2
). On the other hand, the functors introduced in the statement of Theorem
A
are also examples of OTI functors.

To state our next theorem, note that
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
inherits a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action from
\mathscr{C}
. Furthermore, recall that a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathscr{C}
induces an action of an integral form of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}
on
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})
, where
e:=[E]
is the sum of positive Chevalley generators.

Theorem B
.

Let
\varphi
be a CF functor.

(1)

The OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
defines a morphism of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions.

(2)

If
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is given by restriction to
C_{p}
followed by semisimplification, then
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an endomorphism of the mod
p
Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})/(p)
. This endomorphism is given by the central element
e^{p}
in the mod
p
enveloping algebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}
.

The complete statement of this theorem appears in Sections
5.6
and
5.7
. The proof of (1) uses a diagrammatic framework and appears in Section
9
.

1.4.
Strengthening Theorem
A

Let
\mathcal{P}ol=\bigoplus\limits_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{P}ol_{n}
be Friedlander and Suslin’s category of strict polynomial functors (see Section
7
). There is a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathcal{P}ol
, and the Schur–Weyl duality functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}ol\to\mathcal{S}ym
is a morphism of categorical actions. Because the OTI functor is defined purely in terms of the categorical action, we obtain a commutative diagram for any CF functor
\varphi
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}\boxtimes 1}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.}

This allows us to work with the category of polynomial functors, which is a highest weight category, to deduce results about OTI functors on
\mathcal{S}ym
. The following is a special case of Theorem
8.5.3
:

Theorem C
.

Consider a subgroup
H
with
A\subseteq H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
and let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H)^{ss}
be the semisimplification functor.
The OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H)^{ss}
is a symmetric monoidal functor which restricts to the periodic equivalence
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{S}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}
.

Modular branching results are extremely difficult, with one of the most famous results being Kleshchev’s description of branching laws for socles of simple modules
[
34
]
. Theorem
C
can be used to prove a branching result of a different flavour, that is more in the spirit of Cor. 1.1 of
[
2
]
. Indeed, Corollary
8.5.4
says that up to negligible summands, any object in
\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}
restricts to
H
as a trivial module:

Corollary
.

Suppose that
V\in\mathcal{S}_{n,p^{r}}
. Then we have a decomposition

V|_{H}=\mathbf{1}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{i}N_{i},

where each
N_{i}
is indecomposable and
p\mid\operatorname{dim}(N_{i})
for all
i
.

1.5.
Motivation from Lie superalgebras

Representations of Lie superalgebras over the complex numbers are generally not semisimple, even for the general linear Lie superalgebra. One of the most powerful tools for their study is the
Duflo–Serganova (DS)
functor (originally introduced in
[
14
]
, although see
[
23
]
for a more recent survey). The DS functor has been used to describe central characters, understand certain tensor products, construct abelian envelopes of Deligne interpolating categories, and compute superdimensions of irreducible representations (which is the super-analogue of taking dimension mod
p
). The Theorem
A
can be viewed as a modular analogue of the equivalence induced by the
DS
functor in
[
15
]
.

To make the connection to the OTI functor clear, consider a (quasireductive) Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}
has non-semisimple representation theory if and only if it contains a one-dimensional, odd abelian subalgebra
\mathfrak{h}=\mathbb{C}\langle x\rangle
(up to semisimple central extension). One obtains the
DS
functor as follows:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}\mathfrak{g}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Res}_{\mathfrak{h}}}
\scriptstyle{DS_{x}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}\mathfrak{h}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ss}
\textstyle{\operatorname{svec}.}

In this case, the semisimplification functor
ss
takes the particularly simple form
\frac{\operatorname{ker}(x)}{\operatorname{\operatorname{ker}}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x)}
(compare with the formula for OTI functor in Section
3.2
).
We remark that the OTI functor exhibits some of the remarkable behaviour previously observed for
DS
functors, along with new phenomena.

1.6.
Roadmap

In Section
2
we set up preliminary notation and machinery for Deligne tensor products and semisimplification which will be used throughout much of the paper. In Section
3
we recall the Verlinde category
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
and an explicit formula for the semisimplification functor of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}
. Section
4
introduces the OTI functor for finite groups, proves its basic properties, and shows that it categorifies the Glauberman correspondence.

In Section
5
we shift to the categorical setting, defining degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions, with important examples. We introduce the OTI functor in this setting, and precisely state Theorem
B
(1). Sections
6
and
7
contain necessary background on representations of symmetric groups and polynomial functors, and study how the OTI functor behaves on special classes of modules. Schur–Weyl duality, which connects polynomial functors to representations of symmetric groups, is proven to be compatible with the OTI functor. Our work culminates with Section
8
where we prove Theorems
A
and
C
using all the machinery developed prior. Finally, in Section
9
we introduce diagrammatics for the OTI functor which provide a streamlined proof of Theorem
B
.

. We further thank Kevin Coulembier, Eoghan McDowell, and Vera Serganova for many inspiring conversations. We acknowledge Magma for its assistance in computations toward the start of this project. We would also like to thank Ruby, Heinrich and Lukas from the University of

2.
Preliminaries

Let
p
be a prime. We work throughout over an algebraically closed field
\Bbbk
of characteristic
p>0
. In particular, we assume that all categories are
\Bbbk
-linear. We will work exclusively with finite-dimensional representations, writing
\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
for the category of finite dimensional vector spaces, and
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
for the category of finite-dimensional representations of
G
.

2.1.
Locally finite categories

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an essentially small abelian category. We say that
\mathscr{C}
is
locally finite
if every object of
\mathscr{C}
has finite length and hom spaces are finite-dimensional. By a theorem of Takeuchi (
[
16
, Theorem 1.9.15]
),
\mathscr{C}
is locally finite if and only if there exists a
\Bbbk
-coalgebra
C
such that
\mathscr{C}
is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional right comodules over
C
, written
C\operatorname{--}\mathrm{comod}
.

We denote by
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
the 2-category of essentially small
\Bbbk
-linear abelian categories and we write
\mathcal{T}ak
for the 2-subcategory of locally finite abelian categories. The subcategory
\mathcal{T}ak^{\text{ex}}\subset\mathcal{T}ak
has the same objects, but the 1-morphisms are exact functors.

2.2.
Deligne tensor product

Let
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{C}
be essentially small
\Bbbk
-linear abelian categories. In
[
12
, §5.1]
, Deligne defines their tensor product
\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}
, when it exists, to be a
\Bbbk
-linear abelian category admitting a bilinear bifunctor

\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C},\ \ (X,Y)\mapsto X\boxtimes Y,

which is universal in the following sense: for any
\Bbbk
-linear abelian category
\mathscr{D}
, restriction yields an equivalence between the category of right exact
\Bbbk
-linear functors
\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and the category of bilinear bifunctors
\mathscr{B}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
which are right exact in
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{C}
.

Now suppose
\mathscr{C}\simeq C\operatorname{--}\mathrm{comod}
for some
\Bbbk
-coalgebra
C
. Following
[
10
]
, we define the category
C\operatorname{--}\mathrm{comod}_{\mathscr{B}}
as the category of objects
X
in
\mathscr{B}
together with an algebra morphism
C^{*}_{X}\to\operatorname{End}(X)

for some finite-dimensional subcoalgebra
C_{X}\subset C
.
There is a bilinear bifunctor

\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{B}\to C\operatorname{--}\mathrm{comod}_{\mathscr{B}}

and
[
10
, Lemma 3.2.3]
shows we get an equivalence

\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{B}\xrightarrow{\simeq}C-\operatorname{comod}_{\mathscr{B}}.

Using this equivalence,
[
10
, Proposition 3.2.6]
further constructs a pseudo-functor

-\boxtimes-:Tak^{\text{ex}}\times\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at\to\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at

such that
(F\boxtimes\varphi)(X\boxtimes Y)\simeq F(X)\boxtimes\varphi(Y)
for any 1-morphism
F
in
\mathcal{T}ak^{\text{ex}}
and 1-morphism
\varphi
in
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
. The following are immediate consequences.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a locally finite abelian category.

(1)

The pseudo-functor
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes-:\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at\to\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
preserves adjunctions and exactness properties of functors (1-morphisms) in
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
.

(2)

There is a 2-isomorphism in
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at

(F\boxtimes 1)\circ(1\boxtimes\varphi)\simeq(1\boxtimes\varphi)\circ(F\boxtimes 1)

natural for any 1-morphism
F
in
\mathcal{T}ak^{\text{ex}}
and 1-morphism
\varphi
in
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
.

Remark 2.2.2
.

Let us write
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})
for the Grothendieck group of an abelian category
\mathscr{C}
. Recall that for locally finite abelian categories
\mathscr{B},\mathscr{C}
, we have a natural isomorphism

K_{0}(\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C})\cong K_{0}(\mathscr{B})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}K_{0}(\mathscr{C}).

Indeed, all simple objects of
\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}
are of the form
L\boxtimes L^{\prime}
for simple objects
L
of
\mathscr{B}
, and
L^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{C}
. Further,
L_{1}\boxtimes L_{1}^{\prime}\cong L_{2}\boxtimes L_{2}^{\prime}
if and only if
L_{1}\cong L_{2}
and
L_{1}^{\prime}\cong L_{2}^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.2.3
.

There is another class of abelian categories which play an important role in categorical actions, namely those which are
Schurian
. Schurian abelian categories are in some sense dual to locally finite abelian categories, with the main technical difference being that their objects are not all finitely presented (equivalently, compact). To work with them in this paper we would be forced to treat them as a separate case, which seems both undesirable and possibly unmotivated at this stage of our work. Thus we do not consider Schurian categories in our setting. However our understanding is that much of our work carries over to that setting.

If
\mathscr{E}_{1}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{E}_{2}\subseteq\mathscr{D}
are additive subcategories of abelian categories
\mathscr{C},\,\mathscr{D}
, we will at times abuse notation and write
\mathscr{E}_{1}\boxtimes\mathscr{E}_{2}
for the smallest full additive subcategory of
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{D}
containing the objects
X_{1}\boxtimes X_{2}
with
X_{i}\in\mathscr{E}_{i}
.

2.3.
Symmetric tensor categories

An essentially small,
\Bbbk
-linear symmetric monoidal category
(\mathscr{C},\otimes,\mathbf{1})
will be called a
symmetric tensor category
(over
\Bbbk
) if

(1)

\mathscr{C}
is abelian,

(2)

\Bbbk\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{End}(\mathbf{1})
,

(3)

(\mathscr{C},\otimes,\mathbf{1})
is rigid,

(4)

each object of
\mathscr{C}
is of finite length (in particular
\mathscr{C}
is artinian).

In particular, it follows that
\mathscr{C}
is locally finite. Given an object
X
in a tensor category
\mathscr{A}
, we write
X^{*}
for its dual.
It is well known (see
[
12
, §5]
) that if
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{C}
are symmetric tensor categories over an algebraically closed field, then
\mathscr{B}\boxtimes\mathscr{C}
exists and will once again be a symmetric tensor category.

Example 2.3.1
.

The two most significant symmetric tensor categories in this paper are:

(1)

\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G)
, where
G
is a finite group. This is the category of
G
-modules, equivalently modules over the group algebra
\Bbbk G
, or equivalently comodules over the coordinate algebra
\Bbbk[G]:=(\Bbbk G)^{*}
.

(2)

The second category is the Verlinde category
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
, which will be explained in Section
3
.

2.4.
Semisimplification

We now recall the notion of semisimplication, which was originally introduced in
[
4
]
. Our main reference will be
[
18
]
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a symmetric tensor category. Recall that the
trace

\text{tr}(f)\in\Bbbk=\operatorname{End}(\mathbf{1})
of a morphism
f:X\to X
, is given by

\mathbf{1}\to X\otimes X^{*}\xrightarrow{f\otimes 1}X\otimes X^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}X^{*}\otimes X\to\mathbf{1},

where the first map is coevaluation and the last map is evaluation.

The
ideal of negligible morphisms

\mathcal{N}
of
\mathscr{C}
consists of all morphisms
f:X\to Y
such that for any morphism
g:Y\to X
, we have
\text{tr}(fg)=0
.
It is well known that for an indecomposable object
X
, the identity morphism
\operatorname{id}_{X}
is negligible if and only if
\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{id}_{X})=~0
.

By
[
18
, Lem. 2.3]
,
\mathcal{N}
forms a tensor ideal, so the quotient category
\mathscr{C}^{ss}:=\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{N}
is a symmetric monoidal category. We call
\mathscr{C}^{ss}
the
semisimplification
of
\mathscr{C}
, and write

\varphi_{\mathscr{C}}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{ss}

for the canonical quotient functor. By
[
18
, Prop. 2.4]
,
\mathscr{C}^{ss}
is a semisimple tensor category, whose simple objects are given by
\varphi_{\mathscr{C}}(M)
, where
M
runs over all indecomposable objects of
\mathscr{C}
with
\text{tr}(\operatorname{id}_{M})\neq 0
. Note that
\varphi_{\mathscr{C}}
is additive, but not exact in general.

Example 2.4.1
.

If
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G
for a finite group
G
and a field
\Bbbk
of characteristic
p
, we will write
\varphi_{G}
for
\varphi_{\mathscr{C}}
.
For an indecomposable representation
V
,
\operatorname{id}_{V}
is negligible if and only if
\operatorname{dim}V
is a multiple of
p
.
Therefore
(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G)^{ss}
is a semisimple category with simple objects given by
\varphi_{G}(V)
, where
V
runs over all indecomposable
G
-modules of dimension not a multiple of
p
.

By the main theorem of
[
9
]
, there exists a faithful, exact tensor functor
(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G)^{ss}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
. This implies that objects in
(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G)^{ss}
may be viewed as objects in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
with additional structure (a linear action by an algebraic group).

3.
The Verlinde Category

The Verlinde category
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
plays an important role in this paper, and in this section we recall some of the basics about this category and the linear algebraic construction of semisimplification in this setting (see
[
17
]
and references therein).

3.1.
The Verlinde category

We define the
Verlinde category
as the semisimplification
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}:=(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p})^{ss}
(cf. Section
2.4
). This is a semisimple tensor category with simple objects given by
L_{i}:=\varphi_{C_{p}}(M_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq p-1
, where
\varphi_{C_{p}}
denotes the semisimplification functor.
Hence as an abelian category, this category is just
\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
. The decomposition of the tensor product of simple objects
L_{i}
and
L_{j}
is given by the “Verlinde formula”:

L_{i}\otimes L_{j}=\bigoplus\limits_{k=1}^{\min(i,j,p-i,p-j)}L_{|i-j|+2k-1}.

Note that
L_{1}
and
L_{p-1}
generate a copy of
\operatorname{svec}_{\Bbbk}
in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
.

Example 3.1.1
.

If
p=2
,
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
has one simple object,
L_{1}
, and it is equivalent to . If
p=3
,
\operatorname{Ver}_{3}
is equivalent to the category of super vector spaces. The first interesting example is
\operatorname{Ver}_{5}
, which has 4 simple objects. We have

L_{2}\otimes L_{4}\cong L_{3},\ \ \ L_{3}\otimes L_{4}\cong L_{2},\ \ \ L_{3}^{\otimes 2}=L_{3}\oplus L_{1}.

In the Grothendieck ring of
\operatorname{Ver}_{5}
, this last equality becomes
[L_{3}]^{2}-[L_{3}]-[L_{1}]=0
so there cannot exist an additive monoidal functor to vector spaces.

Remark 3.1.2
.

The Grothendieck ring of
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1}][x]/x^{2}
, with
[L_{p-1}]\mapsto x
and
[L_{2}]\mapsto\zeta_{p}+\zeta_{p}^{-1}
.
This description reflects a decomposition
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}\simeq\operatorname{Ver}_{p}^{+}\boxtimes\operatorname{svec}_{\Bbbk}
.

3.2.
A linear algebraic functor

Consider the group algebra
\Bbbk C_{p}=:R\cong\Bbbk[x]/x^{p}
. We view an object of
R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}
as a pair
(V,x)
of a finite-dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector space
V
and an endomorphism
x
satisfying
x^{p}=0
. The indecomposable objects in
R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}
are all of the form

M_{j}:=R/x^{j}=\Bbbk[x]/x^{j}.

The module
M_{1}
is a one dimensional vector space with trivial
x
action, and
M_{p}
is a projective
R
module. All modules
M_{i}
are iterated extensions of
M_{1}
.

We define functors
\varphi_{i}:R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}\to
by the formula:

(3.2.1)

\varphi_{i}(V):=\frac{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})}{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i})}.

One may interpret
\varphi_{i}
as extracting the
M_{i}
isotypic subspace inside
V
:

Lemma 3.2.2
.

The additive functor
\varphi_{i}
applied to
M_{j}
is zero unless
i=j
, and
\varphi_{i}(M_{i})=\Bbbk
.
On morphisms,
\varphi_{i}:\operatorname{End}(M_{i})\to\Bbbk
is the (unique)
\Bbbk
-algebra morphism to the residue field
\Bbbk
.

Proof.

The kernel of
x
in
M_{j}
is always one dimensional, and lies in the image of
x^{i-1}
if and only if
i\leq j
. If we have
i<j
, then this kernel lies in the image of
x^{i}
, so
\varphi_{i}
is nonzero precisely on
M_{i}
. The description of
\varphi_{i}
on morphisms is uniquely determined by the fact that
\varphi_{i}
is
\Bbbk
-linear.
∎

3.3.
The semisimplification functor

We can describe the semsimplification functor
\varphi_{C_{p}}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(C_{p})\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
using the functors
\varphi_{i}
.
To do so, endow
R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}
and
\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
with symmetric tensor structures using the equivalences

\displaystyle R\operatorname{--}\text{mod}\cong\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(C_{p})\quad\text{ and }\quad\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\cong\operatorname{Ver}_{p}.

Then, define
\varphi:R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}\to\prod_{i=1}^{p-1}\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
by

(3.3.1)

V\mapsto\varphi(V):=(\varphi_{1}(V),\varphi_{2}(V),...,\varphi_{p-1}(V))

The following is proven in
[
18
, Prop. 4.8]
.

Proposition 3.3.2
.

The functor
\varphi
is equivalent to the semisimplification functor
\varphi_{C_{p}}
. In particular,
\varphi
is monoidal.

While
\varphi
is not left or right exact, we have the following:

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Suppose that
0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
is a short exact sequence of
R
-modules. The following are equivalent:

(1)

\varphi(X)\to\varphi(Y)
is injective.

(2)

\varphi(Y)\to\varphi(Z)
is surjective.

(3)

0\to\varphi(X)\to\varphi(Y)\to\varphi(Z)\to 0
is exact.

(4)

0\to X\to Y\to Z\to 0
is split as a short exact sequence of
R
-modules.

Proof.

It is clear that (4) implies (3), which implies (1) and (2). Assuming (1), choose decompositions of
X
and
Y
, say
X\cong\oplus_{i}M_{i}^{e_{i}}
and
Y\cong\oplus_{i}M_{i}^{f_{i}}
. Then by injectivity of
\varphi(X)\to\varphi(Y)
, the induced map

M_{i}^{e_{i}}\to X\to Y\to M_{i}^{f_{i}}

is a split monomorphism. So we may define splittings on each summand of this decomposition of
Y
, giving a map
\pi:Y\to X
, whose composition
X\to Y\xrightarrow{\pi}X
is equal to the identity modulo the radical of
\operatorname{End}(X)
. This composition is thus an isomorphism, so the inclusion of
X
into
Y
is split. An analogous argument shows that (2) implies (4).
∎

Remark 3.3.4
.

For an
R
-module
M
, the Tate cohomology of
M
is defined by

\operatorname{ker}(x)/\operatorname{im}(x^{p-1}).

This carries a filtration by the images of
x^{i}
, and the associated graded pieces of this filtration are our components
\varphi_{i}
.
This description allows for the general expression of exactness for the components
\varphi_{i}
: they are associated graded parts of a natural filtration on zero-th Tate cohomology for the action of
x
. In general, one may understand the exactness properties of
\varphi
as arising from a filtration by images of
x
on the cohomology of the two periodic complex

M\xrightarrow{x}M\xrightarrow{x^{p-1}}M\to\dots\ .

4.
OTI for finite groups

In this section, we introduce the One Tree Island (OTI) functor, in the concrete case of representations of a finite group.

4.1.
Representations in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}

Let
G
be a finite group. We write
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
for the category of
G
-modules in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
. The objects of this category are
\mathbf{V}\in\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
equipped with an algebra map
\Bbbk G\to\operatorname{End}(\mathbf{V})
. Note that
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
is equivalent to the Deligne tensor product

\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G\cong\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p},

hence every object
\mathbf{V}
of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
decomposes as

\mathbf{V}=(V_{1}\boxtimes L_{1})\oplus(V_{2}\boxtimes L_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus(V_{p-1}\boxtimes L_{p-1}),

where
V_{1},\dots,V_{p-1}
are representations of
G
.
The indecomposable objects are then of the form
V\boxtimes L_{i}
, where
V
is an indecomposable
G
-module.
Morphisms between objects are determined by

\operatorname{Hom}(V\boxtimes L_{i},V^{\prime}\boxtimes L_{j})\cong\begin{cases}\operatorname{Hom}(V,V^{\prime})&\text{ if }i=j,\\
0&\text{ if }i\neq j.\end{cases}

Similarly, the tensor product on
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
is given by:

(V\boxtimes L_{i})\otimes(V^{\prime}\boxtimes L_{j})=(V\otimes V^{\prime})\boxtimes(L_{i}\otimes L_{j}).

Consider the natural inclusion of tensor categories
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}\subseteq\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
, given by endowing an object in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
with the trivial action of
G
.
The tensor product then induces an exact functor

(4.1.1)

\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G.

4.2.
OTI functors for finite groups

In characteristic
p
, the representation theory of a finite group is not semisimple if and only if the order of the group
|G|
is divisible by
p
. By Cauchy’s theorem, this occurs exactly when
G
contains a cyclic group
H
of order
p
.

Let
H
be such a cyclic subgroup of
G
.
Consider the composition of the restriction functor with semisimplification:

\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Res}_{H}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\xrightarrow{\varphi_{H}}\operatorname{Ver}_{p}.

Note that the centraliser
C_{G}(H)
naturally acts on each component
\varphi_{i}(V)
(see (
3.2.1
)) of
\varphi_{H}(V)
with trivial
H
action, and hence
\Phi_{H}(V)
naturally carries an action of
C_{G}(H)/H
. The
OTI functor for finite groups
is given by:

\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}C_{G}(H)/H.

Note we may upgrade the source to get a functor

\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G\cong\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G)\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{H}\boxtimes 1}\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(C_{G}(H)/H)\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn action verp on repG})}\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}C_{G}(H)/H,

which we also denote by
\Phi_{H}
.
Explicitly,
\Phi_{H}(M\boxtimes L_{i})=\Phi_{H}(M)\otimes L_{i}
, where
\Phi_{H}(M)
is a
C_{G}(H)/H
-module in
\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
.

Remark 4.2.1
.

The functor
\Phi_{H}
semisimplifies the action of the cyclic subgroup
H
, hence
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{G}(H)/H
is “closer” to being semisimple.
Now set
G_{1}:=G
,
H_{1}:=H
, and
G_{2}~:=~C_{G}(H)/H
. If
p\operatorname{\big|}|G_{2}|
, choose a cyclic subgroup
H_{2}\subseteq G_{2}
of order
p
, and let
G_{3}:=C_{G_{2}}(H_{2})/H_{2}
. Continuing like this until
(|G_{n}|,p)=1
, we obtain symmetric monoidal functors

\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G_{1}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{H_{1}}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G_{2}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{H_{2}}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G_{3}\xrightarrow{}\cdots\xrightarrow{\Phi_{H_{n-1}}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G_{n},

where
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G_{n}
is
semisimple
. Note that this composition of functors depends on the choices of cyclic subgroups at each step.
The above iteration procedure has been much exploited in the super world, and was used to the compute the superdimensions of irreducible representations (see for instance
[
22
]
and
[
25
]
).

Example 4.2.2
.

Let
G=S_{n}
for
n\geq p
, and let
H\subseteq S_{n}
denote the subgroup generated by the
p
-cycle
(n-p+1,\dots,n)
. Then we obtain an OTI functor
\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}S_{n-p}
.
Applying this iteratively as above, we get a functor
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}S_{n}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}S_{r}
, where
n=qp+r
for
r<p
. Note that
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}S_{r}
is semisimple.

We collect some properties of
\Phi_{H}
. First, for
\mathbf{V}=\bigoplus_{i}(V_{i}\boxtimes L_{i})\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G
, define

(4.2.3)

\operatorname{dim}_{p}\mathbf{V}:=\operatorname{dim}(V_{1})+2\operatorname{dim}(V_{2})+\dots+(p-1)\operatorname{dim}(V_{p-1})\quad\text{ (mod }p)\in\mathbb{F}_{p}.

Note that
\operatorname{dim}_{p}\mathbf{V}=\operatorname{tr}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{V}})
(see Section
2.4
), the categorical dimension of
\mathbf{V}
.

Lemma 4.2.4
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(C_{G}(H)/H)
satisfies the following properties:

\operatorname{(i)}

\Phi_{H}
is
\Bbbk
-linear and symmetric monoidal;

\operatorname{(ii)}

\operatorname{dim}_{p}\Phi_{H}(M)\equiv\operatorname{dim}M\quad(\operatorname{mod}p)
;

\operatorname{(iii)}

\Phi_{H}(M)=0
if and only if
\operatorname{Res}_{H}M
is a projective (equivalently, free)
H
-module;

\operatorname{(iv)}

\Phi_{H}(P)=0
for any projective
G
-module.

\operatorname{(v)}

If
X
is a
G
-set and we write
\Bbbk[X]
for the corresponding permutation module, then

\Phi_{H}(\Bbbk[X])=\Bbbk[X^{H}]\boxtimes L_{1}.

Proof.

Property (i) follows because
\Phi_{H}
is a composition of restriction and semisimplification, which are both symmetric monoidal functors. Property (ii) holds because symmetric monoidal functors preserve the trace of an endomorphism and
\text{tr}(\operatorname{id}_{M})\equiv\operatorname{dim}M
mod
p
. Property (iii) follows from Lemma
3.2.2
, and (iv) follows immediately from (iii).

For (v), we may decompose
X=\bigcup\limits_{i}\mathcal{O}_{i}
into a union of
H
-orbits, and obtain a corresponding decomposition

\Bbbk[X]=\bigoplus\limits_{i}\Bbbk[\mathcal{O}_{i}]
of
H
-modules, with each
\Bbbk[\mathcal{O}_{i}]
an indecomposable
H
-module of dimension
|\mathcal{O}_{i}|
. Since
H
is a
p
-group, all nontrivial orbits are of size a multiple of
p
, hence are killed by
\Phi_{H}
, leaving us with only trivial orbits, i.e.
\Bbbk[X^{H}]
.
∎

Remark 4.2.5
.

On summands of permutation modules,
\Phi_{H}
lands in the
L_{1}
-component of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(G)
, and Lemma
4.2.4
(v) shows that
\Phi_{H}
agrees with the Brauer construction (Tate cohomology) on these modules.

Remark 4.2.6
.

There is another approach to obtaining the action of
C_{G}(H)
on
\Phi_{H}
which is more in the spirit of Hopf algebras. Recall that a
G
-module is simply a module over the group algebra
\Bbbk G
, which is itself a cocommutative Hopf algebra. Since
\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is symmetric monoidal,
\Phi_{H}(\Bbbk G)
is once again a cocommutative Hopf algebra, where
H
acts on
\Bbbk G
by conjugation. Further, for any
G
-module
M
,
\Phi_{H}(M)
is naturally a module over
\Phi_{H}(\Bbbk G)
. Using (v) of Lemma
4.2.4
, it is not difficult to show that
\Phi_{H}(\Bbbk G)
is canonically identified, as a Hopf algebra, with
\Bbbk C_{G}(H)
.

4.3.
OTI functors from shifted cyclic subgroups

We continue to let
G
be a finite group. We may generalize our construction above by considering subalgebras
R
of
\Bbbk G
isomorphic to
\Bbbk[x]/(x^{p})
, and applying the functor

\Phi_{R}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Res}_{R}}R\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\operatorname{Ver}_{p},

with
\varphi
defined as in (
3.3.1
). In this case,
\Phi_{R}(M)
admits a natural action of the centralizer of
x
in
\Bbbk G
, and
\Phi_{R}(M)=0
if and only if
M
is free over
R
.

One case of particular interest are the subalgebras arising from shifted cyclic subgroups, which we now briefly recall (see
[
5
, Sec. 5.8]
).

Let
A\cong C_{p}^{r}
be an elementary abelian
p
-group, and write

\Bbbk A=\Bbbk[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}]/(x_{1}^{p},\dots,x_{r}^{p})

where
1-x_{1},\dots,1-x_{r}
are generators for
A\cong C_{p}^{r}
. A
shifted cyclic subgroup

R_{\mathbf{a}}
of
A
is a subalgebra of
\Bbbk A
generated by an element of the form
z_{\mathbf{a}}:=a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{r}x_{r}
, where
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})\in\Bbbk^{r}
is non-zero. Thus
R_{\mathbf{a}}\cong\Bbbk[z_{\mathbf{a}}]/(z_{\mathbf{a}}^{p})
. Note that
R_{\mathbf{a}}
will not be the group algebra of a subgroup of
A
in general.

Shifted cyclic subgroups correspond to the closed points of
\text{Spec}H^{\bullet}(A,\Bbbk)
(which happens to be the Balmer spectrum
[
3
]
of
\mathbf{D}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}A)
). If
M
is a finite-dimensional
A
-module, then
\Phi_{R}(M)=0
for all shifted cyclic subgroups
R
of
A
if and only if
M
is a projective (equivalently, free)
A
-module. Combined with Chouinard’s Lemma
[
5
]
, which states that a module over a general finite group
G
is projective if and only if it is so over every elementary abelian subgroup, the OTI functors
\Phi_{R}
can detect projectivity of any finite-dimensional
G
-module.

4.4.
Morphism on mod
p
Grothendieck rings

We again let
H\subseteq G
be a cyclic subgroup of order
p
.
The functor
\Phi_{H}
is not exact, so does not induce a morphism on Grothendieck groups. Nevertheless, a weaker statement, shown below, still holds.
Throughout, we will write
\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C}):=K_{0}(\mathscr{C})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{p}
for the
\operatorname{mod}p
Grothendieck group of an abelian category
\mathscr{C}
.
Note that
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a
K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p})
-algebra via the morphism
\operatorname{dim}_{p}
(see (
4.2.3
)), and we have an isomorphism

\displaystyle K_{0}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}G)\otimes_{K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p})}\mathbb{F}_{p}

\displaystyle\cong(K_{0}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p}))\otimes_{K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p})}\mathbb{F}_{p}

\displaystyle\cong\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G).

Since the semisimplification functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is symmetric monoidal, it preserves categorical dimension, meaning that
\operatorname{dim}_{p}\varphi(M)\equiv\operatorname{dim}M
(mod
p
). It follows that the assignment

\phi:\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p})\to\mathbb{F}_{p},\quad[X]\mapsto\operatorname{dim}_{p}\varphi(X)

is well-defined and agrees with the algebra morphism

\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p})\to\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{vec})=\mathbb{F}_{p}

induced by the exact functor
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{vec}
, forgetting the
C_{p}
-action. The following is an easy corollary, and is proved more generally in Proposition
5.7.1
.

Proposition 4.4.1
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(C_{G}(H)/H)

induces an algebra morphism on
\operatorname{mod}p
Grothendieck groups,

\phi_{H}:\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}G)\to\overline{K_{0}}(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{G}(H)/H),\quad[M]\mapsto[\Phi_{H}(M)]

Under the natural identification
\overline{K_{0}}(C_{G}(H))\cong\overline{K_{0}}(C_{G}(H)/H)
, we have

\phi_{H}([M])=[\operatorname{Res}_{C_{G}(H)}(M)].

Remark 4.4.2
.

Note that Proposition
4.4.1
has a well-known analogue in the super setting, first observed in
[
31
]
(see
[
23
]
for a general description in terms of Grothendieck rings).

4.5.
Application: Glauberman Correspondence

In this section, we use the OTI functor to categorify the cyclic case of the Glauberman correspondence
[
21
]
, which is a bijection relating irreducible characters of finite groups. Our categorification
provides a natural interpretation for the sign that appears in this bijection. First we recall the statement.

Theorem 4.5.1
.

Let
H
be a group of order
p
acting on a finite group
G
of order coprime to
p
. Then there exists a canonical bijection between the (characteristic zero) irreducible representations of
G
fixed by
H
and the irreducible representations of the invariants
G^{H}
:

V\mapsto\widehat{V}

Up to signs, this correspondence is induced by the restriction map on Grothendieck rings mod
p
:

(4.5.2)

[\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{G^{H}}(V)]\equiv\pm[\widehat{V}]\quad\mathrm{mod}\ p.

As the orders of
G
and
G^{H}
are coprime to
p
, the representation theory of these groups in characteristic
p
is semisimple, with irreducible representations mirroring those in characteristic
0
.
Therefore, we may just as well interpret the Glauberman correspondence as a bijection relating simple modules in characteristic
p
.

Moreover, by Clifford theory (see Theorem 4 of
[
1
]
), for any simple module
V
of
G
fixed by
H
, there is a unique simple module
\tilde{V}
of
G\rtimes H
restricting to
V
. Let
\Phi_{G\rtimes H,H}
denote the OTI functor for
H
in
G\rtimes H
and note that
C_{G\rtimes H}(H)/H\cong G^{H}
.

Theorem 4.5.3
.

In characteristic
p
, the OTI functor

\Phi_{G\rtimes H,H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G\rtimes H)\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(G^{H})

categorifies the Glauberman correspondence: it takes the extension of an
H
-fixed simple module
V
in
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G\rtimes H)
to its Glauberman correspondent
\widehat{V}
, in the component determined by the sign mod
p
:

\Phi_{G\rtimes H,H}(\tilde{V})=\widehat{V}\boxtimes L_{\pm 1}

Remark 4.5.4
.

Note that on the level of vector spaces, the Glauberman correspondence may also be induced by the Brauer construction for
H
. By involving the Verlinde category we provide a new categorical interpretation of the sign that appears in the correspondence. Our approach also illustrates an extra monoidal compatibility of this map.

The proof is a corollary of a compatibility between induction and the OTI functor:

Lemma 4.5.5
.

Let
H
be a group of order
p
acting on a group
G
of order coprime to
p
, with centraliser
G^{H}
. Then as functors from
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(G^{H}\times H)
to
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(G^{H})
we have:

\Phi_{G\rtimes H,H}\circ\operatorname{Ind}^{G\rtimes H}_{G^{H}\times H}\cong\Phi_{G^{H}\times H,H}.

Proof.

This induction splits as a direct sum of subspaces for the cosets of
G^{H}
in
G
, and the action of
H
permutes these. As
H
and
G
are coprime, the action of
H
on
G/G^{H}
fixes only the trivial coset of
G^{H}
(see Lemma 3 of
[
21
]
). Thus, the action of
H
on this decomposition fixes only the trivial component, inducing the desired identification of functors.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.5.3
.

First note that the index of
G^{H}\times H
in
G\rtimes H
is coprime to
p
, so for any
G
module
U
, the canonical map

\operatorname{Ind}^{G\rtimes H}_{G^{H}\times H}\mathrm{Res}^{G\rtimes H}_{G^{H}\times H}(U)\to U

is split surjective. From this, we see that every simple module of
G\rtimes H
is a direct summand of the induction of an indecomposable module of
G^{H}\times H
. By uniserial theory (see
[
1
]
), these indecomposable modules all have the form
W\boxtimes M_{j}
for
W
an irreducible module of
G^{H}
. So for
U
a simple
G\rtimes H
module, there exists some
W
and
M_{j}
such that

U\subset_{\oplus}\operatorname{Ind}^{G\rtimes H}_{G^{H}\times H}(W\boxtimes M_{j}).

Now with
\Phi\coloneqq\Phi_{G\rtimes H,H}
, we have the split inclusion:

\Phi(U)\subset_{\oplus}\Phi\operatorname{Ind}^{G\rtimes H}_{G^{H}\times H}(W\boxtimes M_{j})\cong\Phi(W\boxtimes M_{j})\cong W\boxtimes L_{j}.

This shows for simple
U
, the object
\Phi(U)
is either simple or zero in
\operatorname{Rep}_{\operatorname{Ver}_{p}}(G^{H})
. As the Glauberman correspondence (with corresponding sign) is determined by restriction in mod
p
Grothendieck groups, Proposition
4.4.1
completes the proof.
∎

5.
OTI functors on categorical Heisenberg actions

We discuss the OTI functor in the setting of categorical Heisenberg actions.

5.1.
CF functors

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and fix a subgroup
H\subseteq S_{n}
.
Recall that a subgroup
H^{\prime}\subseteq S_{n}
is
transitive
if its action on
\{1,\dots,n\}
is transitive.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be an abelian category.
A
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
is a
CF functor
for
H\subseteq S_{n}
if
\varphi(\operatorname{Ind}_{H^{\prime}}^{H}M)=0
for all non-transitive subgroups
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
and all
H^{\prime}
-modules
M
.

We note that Definition
5.1.1
allows for
\varphi
to be the zero functor, but we will usually impose an extra nontriviality assumption on
\varphi
(see also Lemma
5.1.6
).

Example 5.1.2
.

If
n=p
,
C_{p}
is the unique minimal transitive subgroup of
S_{p}
up to conjugacy. A
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\mathscr{A}
is a CF functor for
C_{p}\subset S_{p}
if and only if
\varphi
vanishes on projective modules. There are several examples of such functors:

(1)

We may take
\mathscr{A}=\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
and
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
the semisimplification functor. Then
\varphi
is given, componentwise, by the formulas in (
3.2.1
). In this case
\varphi
is symmetric monoidal.

(2)

Take
\mathscr{A}=\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
and let
\varphi=\varphi_{1}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\mathscr{A}

(see (
3.2.1
)). This choice of CF functor is a direct summand of the one in example (1). It is no longer monoidal, although it is lax monoidal.

(3)

Take
\mathscr{A}=\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
and let
\varphi(V)=\operatorname{ker}(x)/\operatorname{im}(x^{p})
, where we follow the notation of Section
3.2
. This CF functor is the well-known Brauer construction.

Example 5.1.3
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a locally finite abelian category.
Suppose
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
is a CF functor for
H\subseteq S_{n}
. Write
\mathbf{1}
for the trivial representation in
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
as usual.
Set
\varphi_{\mathbf{1}}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
to be

\varphi_{\mathbf{1}}(M)=\frac{M^{H}}{M^{H}\cap\left(\sum\limits_{h\in H}\operatorname{im}(1-h)\right)}.

It is straightforward to check that
\varphi_{\mathbf{1}}
is a CF functor, and that we have a noncanonical, split inclusion
\varphi_{\mathbf{1}}(M)\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1})\to\varphi(M)
for any
H
-module
M
.

Example 5.1.4
.

Let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
be a CF functor for
H\subseteq S_{n}
. If
\mathscr{A}
is additionally locally finite (which is our main case of interest), then it admits an exact conservative functor
\omega:\mathscr{A}\to\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
, and
\omega\circ\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
will be another CF functor.

Remark 5.1.5
.

In
[
10
]
, Coulembier and Flake introduce a very similar notion, which they call a
\mathbf{V}
-functor. The difference to our setup is that in
loc. cit.
it is assumed that
\mathscr{A}
is a tensor category and that
\varphi
is monoidal. We drop this monoidality assumption as it is less natural in our setting.
In particular, if
\mathscr{A}
and
\varphi
are monoidal, then the condition to be a CF functor may be weakened to
\varphi(\operatorname{Ind}_{H^{\prime}}^{H}\mathbf{1})=0
, as is done in
[
10
]
.

The following is a slight generalization of
[
10
, Lem. 5.1.3]
.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

The following are equivalent for a subgroup
H\subseteq S_{n}
:

(1)

There exists a nonzero CF functor for
H\subseteq S_{n}
;

(2)

The semisimplification functor
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H)^{ss}
is a CF functor;

(3)

There is a
p
-subgroup
P\subseteq H
which is transitive in
S_{n}
;

(4)

n
is a power of
p
, and
H
is a transitive subgroup of
S_{n}
.

Proof.

We may use the same proof as given in
[
10
]
, only the direction
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
needs a small adjustment. For this, let
P\subseteq H
be a Sylow subgroup. Then for any
H
-module
M
,
M
splits off
\operatorname{Ind}_{P}^{H}M
. Since
\varphi
is nonzero, this forces
P
to be a transitive subgroup.
∎

Remark 5.1.7
.

If
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
is a CF functor for
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
, then
\tilde{\varphi}:=\varphi\circ\operatorname{Res}_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}\to\mathscr{A}
is also a CF functor. Thus without loss of generality we may assume all CF functors have
H=S_{p^{r}}
. However we will work with more general subgroups, mostly for conceptual clarity.

5.2.
Categorical Heisenberg actions

In the following, we mainly follow the conventions and notation developed in
[
7
]
. Let
\mathscr{C}
be a locally finite abelian category. A
degenerate categorical Heisenberg action
on
\mathscr{C}
is the data of:

(1)

An integer
k\in\mathbb{Z}
(the
central charge
).

(2)

Biadjoint endofunctors
E,F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
(in particular,
E
and
F
are exact). This gives rise to units and counits of adjunctions, which we denote as follows:

\eta_{1}:\mathbf{1}\to FE,\hskip 10.00002pt\eta_{2}:\mathbf{1}\to EF,

\epsilon_{1}:EF\to\mathbf{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\epsilon_{2}:FE\to\mathbf{1}.

(3)

A natural transformation
x:E\to E
.

(4)

A natural transformation
T:E^{2}\to E^{2}
.

This data is subject to a number of relations, which are explained in
[
7
, Sec. 3]
, as well as in Section
9.2
.
One consequence of these relations is that the natural transformation
T
induces an action of
S_{n}
on
E^{n}
via
s_{i}\mapsto E^{n-i-1}TE^{i-1}
, where
s_{i}
is the simple transposition
(i,i+1)
. Another consequence is that in the Grothendieck group we have the Heisenberg relation
[E][F]-[F][E]=k
.

Now suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is another locally finite abelian category. Then by Lemma
2.2.1
, the Deligne tensor product
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
inherits a natural degenerate categorical Heisenberg action. For example, our biadjoint functors will be
E\boxtimes 1
and
F\boxtimes 1
.

Example 5.2.1
.

Representations of symmetric groups provide the central example of a categorical Heisenberg action in this paper. Let
\mathcal{S}ym=\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
. For
M\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
, set

E=\bigoplus\limits_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{Res}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}\quad\text{and}\quad F=\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n}}^{S_{n+1}}.

Then
E
and
F
are biadjoint functors on
\mathcal{S}ym
. We have an endomorphism
x:E\to E
given on
\operatorname{Res}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}
by the action of the Jucys–Murphy element
x=(1,n)+(2,n)+\dots+(n-1,n)\in\Bbbk E
.
There is a natural transformation
T
on
E^{2}
given by right multiplication by the simple transposition
s_{n-1}
on the component
\operatorname{Res}^{S_{n}}_{S_{n-2}}
.
This data defines a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathcal{S}ym
of central charge
-1
(see
[
37
]
for more details), which categorifies the Fock space representation of the Heisenberg algebra.

5.3.
Morphisms of categorical actions

Suppose that
(\mathscr{C},E,F,T,x,\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}),
and
(\mathscr{D},E^{\prime},F^{\prime},T^{\prime},x^{\prime},\eta_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{2}^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}^{\prime},\epsilon_{2}^{\prime})
are locally finite abelian categories with degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions of the same central charge.

Definition 5.3.1
.

A morphism of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions is the data of a functor
\Psi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
and natural isomorphisms

\zeta_{E}:\Psi E\xrightarrow{\sim}E^{\prime}\Psi,\ \ \ \zeta_{F}:\Psi F\xrightarrow{\sim}F^{\prime}\Psi,

such that the following diagrams commute:

(1)

.

\textstyle{\Psi E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi x}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\Psi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}\Psi}
\textstyle{\Psi E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{E}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\Psi}

(2)

.

\textstyle{\Psi E^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{E}E}
\scriptstyle{\Psi T}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\Psi E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E^{\prime}\zeta_{E}}
\textstyle{(E^{\prime})^{2}\Psi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T^{\prime}\Psi}
\textstyle{\Psi E^{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{E}E}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\Psi E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E^{\prime}\zeta_{E}}
\textstyle{(E^{\prime})^{2}\Psi}

(3)

.

\textstyle{\Psi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi\eta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{1}^{\prime}\Psi}
\textstyle{\Psi FE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{F}E}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}\Psi E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}\zeta_{E}}
\textstyle{F^{\prime}E^{\prime}\Psi}

(4)

.

\textstyle{\Psi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi\eta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{2}^{\prime}\Psi}
\textstyle{\Psi EF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{E}F}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}\Psi F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E^{\prime}\zeta_{F}}
\textstyle{E^{\prime}F^{\prime}\Psi}

5.4.
Categorical Kac–Moody action

By the biadjunction between
E
and
F
, the natural transformation
x:E\to E
induces a natural transformation
y:F\to F
. By the assumptions made on
\mathscr{C}
, the actions of
x
and
y
on
E(M)
and
F(M)
will be locally finite for any object
M
of
\mathscr{C}
. Thus
E(M)
and
F(M)
admit generalized eigenspace decompositions which are natural in
M
. This implies we obtain a decomposition of functors:

E=\bigoplus_{i\in\Bbbk}E_{i},\ \ \ F=\bigoplus\limits_{i\in\Bbbk}F_{i}.

Let
I
be the set of eigenvalues that appear in these decompositions. There is an associated Kac–Moody algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
constructed from
I
(see
[
7
]
for the precise definition). The main result of
[
7
]
is that this gives rise to a categorical Kac–Moody representation of
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
on
\mathscr{C}
. Decategorifying induces an action of
\mathfrak{g}_{I}
on
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})
, where
E_{i}
and
F_{i}
decategorify to the Chevalley generators.
In cases of interest to us below,
I=\mathbb{F}_{p}
and the Kac–Moody algebra is
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}}_{p}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}_{p}(\mathbb{C}[t,t^{-1}])\oplus\mathbb{C}c
.

Example 5.4.1
.

The categorical Heisenberg action of Example
5.2.1
induces an action of
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}}_{p}^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{S}ym
. The Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{S}ym
then provides an integral form for the basic representation of
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}\mathfrak{l}}_{p}^{\prime}
:

K_{0}(\mathcal{S}ym)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}\cong L(\Lambda_{0}).

5.5.
OTI functor on categorical actions

Let
H
be a subgroup of
S_{p^{r}}
, so that by restriction we obtain a homomorphism
\Bbbk H\to\operatorname{End}(E^{p^{r}})
. It follows that we may view
E^{p^{r}}
as defining a functor:

E^{p^{r}}:\mathscr{C}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathscr{C}}(H)\simeq\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H.

Definition 5.5.1
.

Let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
be a CF functor for
H\subset S_{p^{r}}
. Given a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathscr{C}
, define the
OTI functor

\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
by the composition:

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{E^{p^{r}}}\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\xrightarrow{1\boxtimes\varphi}\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.

Remark 5.5.2
.

Note that the roles of
E
and
F
may be interchanged in the definition of a categorical Heisenberg action, so we could also define an OTI functor in terms of
F
.

We now provide examples of OTI functors for different, well-known categorical actions.

Example 5.5.3
.

We continue to study
\mathcal{S}ym
as in Examples
5.2.1
and
5.4.1
.
Let
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
, and let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
be a CF functor. Then we obtain an OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{S}ym\to\mathcal{S}ym\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
whose components are given by the following composition:

\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\xrightarrow{E^{p^{r}}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\xrightarrow{1\boxtimes\varphi}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.

Here, the first arrow is simply the restriction functor to the subgroup
S_{n-p^{r}}\times H\subseteq S_{n}
. In particular, if
r=1
and
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
is the semisimplification functor, then
\Phi_{\varphi}
agrees with the OTI functor in Example
4.2.2
.
This example is the main topic of Section
6
.

Example 5.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{P}ol
be the category of strict polynomial functors. Objects in
\mathcal{P}ol
are functors
M:\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\to\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
such that the induced map on morphisms is polynomial (see Section
7.1
for the precise definition). The category
\mathcal{P}ol
admits a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action, originally defined in
[
30
]
, see also Section
7.1
. It was proven in
[
30
]
that the Schur–Weyl duality functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}ol\to\mathcal{S}ym
is a morphism of categorical actions, and we will use this in Section
7.3
to show that
\mathcal{F}
intertwines the OTI functors on each category.

We provide a final example in which the OTI functor exhibits a very different behavior.

Example 5.5.5
.

Let
V
be a vector space, and consider the category of algebraic representations
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}GL(V)
. We have a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action of central charge
0
given by:

E(M)=V\otimes M,\ \ \ F(M)=V^{*}\otimes M.

We refer to the introduction of
[
7
]
for more details and references.
Let
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
and choose a CF functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
. Then we have

\Phi_{\varphi}(M)=V^{(r)}\otimes M\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1}),

where
V^{(r)}
denotes the
r
th Frobenius twist of
V
. (If we define the OTI functor in terms of
F
, then we obtain

\Phi_{\varphi}(M)=(V^{*})^{(r)}\otimes M\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1})
.)

Two phenomena of note appear in this example. One is that
\Phi_{\varphi}
only depends on
\varphi(\mathbf{1})
and the power of
p
.
The second is that
\Phi_{\varphi}
is exact. The exactness of
\Phi_{\varphi}
is notable, as it holds for all known (to the authors) examples of categorical Heisenberg actions of central charge 0.

Remark 5.5.6
.

Consider the setting of Example
5.5.5
. Let
A_{0}
denote the (unshifted) fundamental alcove,
\lambda_{r}
the
r
th Steinberg weight, and
\mathfrak{X}_{r}=\lambda_{r}+p^{r}A_{0}
. Then for
\lambda\in\mathfrak{X}_{r}
, the Donkin tensor product theorem (
[
13
]
) implies that

T_{\lambda}\otimes V^{(r)}\cong T_{\lambda+p^{r}\varpi_{1}},

where
T_{\mu}
denotes in the indecomposable tilting module of highest weight
\mu
.
This isomorphism is suggestive of a periodic equivalence in this setting, in the spirit of those we study for
\mathcal{S}ym
and
\mathcal{P}ol
further on in the paper.

5.6.
OTI functors commute with the categorical action

Categorical actions are both rich and highly rigid. For instance, we saw in Example
5.4.1
that
\mathcal{S}ym
categorifies an integral form of the basic representation of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}^{\prime}
, which is irreducible over the complex numbers. Irreducibility is an immediate obstruction to the existence of a nontrivial exact functor which commutes with the categorical action.

However, it is still possible for a
non-exact
functor to commute with the categorical action. Furthermore, the mod
p
enveloping algebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}^{\prime}
admits a very large centre, and the mod
p
reduction of the basic representation is reducible (see
[
11
]
). In light of Proposition
4.4.1
, one might hope that there in an OTI functor which categorifies a central element of the mod
p
center, and realizes an endomorphism of the mod
p
reduction of the basic representation.

We will now explain that there is an OTI functor which indeed does all of the above. To this end, suppose that
\mathscr{C}
admits a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action, and let
\mathscr{A}
be a locally finite abelian category. As explained in Section
5.2
,
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
will inherit the natural structure of a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action of the same central charge. In Section
9
, we prove the following remarkable theorem.

Theorem 5.6.1
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
defines a morphism of categorical actions, where the natural isomorphisms

\zeta_{E}:\Phi_{\varphi}\circ E\xrightarrow{\sim}(E\boxtimes 1)\circ\Phi_{\varphi},\ \ \ \zeta_{F}:\Phi_{\varphi}\circ F\xrightarrow{\sim}(F\boxtimes 1)\circ\Phi_{\varphi},

are explicitly described in Section
9.4
.

Theorem
5.6.1
immediately implies the following:

Corollary 5.6.2
.

For
i\in\Bbbk
, we have natural isomorphisms

\Phi_{\varphi}\circ E_{i}\cong(E_{i}\boxtimes 1)\circ\Phi_{\varphi},\ \ \ \Phi_{\varphi}\circ F_{i}\cong(F_{i}\boxtimes 1)\circ\Phi_{\varphi}.

Remark 5.6.3
.

For the reader interested in functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}
which are morphisms of categorical actions, this amounts to the case
\mathscr{A}=\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
, and many examples of such CF functors were provided in Examples
5.1.3
and
5.1.4
.

5.7.
OTI functors and mod p Grothendieck rings

Proposition 5.7.1
.

Consider the semisimplification functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
.
Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be locally finite abelian categories and consider an exact functor
S:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(C_{p})
. Then the composition

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{S}\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(C_{p})\xrightarrow{1\boxtimes\varphi}\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}

induces a morphism on
\operatorname{mod}p
Grothendieck groups,

\displaystyle\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C})\to\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{D})\cong K_{0}(\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p})\otimes_{K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p})}\mathbb{F}_{p}:\quad[X]\mapsto[(1\boxtimes\varphi)S(X)],

that agrees with the morphism induced by the exact functor

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{S}\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(C_{p})\to\mathscr{D}\boxtimes\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{D}.

Proof.

In view of the explicit formula for
\varphi
(see (
3.3.1
)), it suffices to prove the following claim: for any object
X\in\mathscr{D}
with endomorphism
x
satisfying
x^{p}=0
, we have that

[X]\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}i\cdot\bigg[\frac{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})}{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i})}\bigg]\hskip 10.00002pt{(\operatorname{mod}p)}.

in the mod
p
Grothendieck group of
\mathscr{D}
.

The short exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})\to\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})\to\operatorname{im}(x^{i})\to 0

valid for
1\leq i\leq p-1
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}i\cdot\bigg[\frac{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})}{\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}i\cdot[\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})]-i\cdot[\operatorname{ker}(x)\cap\operatorname{im}(x^{i})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}i\cdot\bigg([\operatorname{im}(x^{i-1})]-2[\operatorname{im}(x^{i})]+[\operatorname{im}(x^{i+1})]\bigg).

The coefficient of
[\operatorname{im}(x^{i})]
in this sum is zero if
1\leq i\leq p-2
, so this sum telescopes to the desired equality mod
p
:

[\operatorname{im}(x^{0})]+(p-2)[\operatorname{im}(x^{p-1})]-2(p-1)[\operatorname{im}(x^{p-1})]\equiv[X]\hskip 10.00002pt{(\operatorname{mod}p)}.

∎

Suppose now
\mathscr{C}
admits a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action. We write
e:\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C})\to\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C})
for the morphism on mod
p
Grothendieck groups induced by
E
. The following corollary follows immediately from the proposition.

Corollary 5.7.2
.

Let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
be given by semisimplification.
The OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}

induces a morphism

\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C})\to\overline{K_{0}}(\mathscr{C})\cong K_{0}(\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p})\otimes_{K_{0}(\operatorname{Ver}_{p})}\mathbb{F}_{p}:\quad[X]\mapsto[\Phi_{\varphi}(X)],

that agrees with
e^{p}
.

Example 5.7.3
.

Returning to
\mathcal{S}ym
from Examples
5.2.1
,
5.4.1
, and
5.5.3
, recall that
K_{0}(\mathcal{S}ym)
is an integral form of the basic representation for
\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}_{p}}
. Thus
K_{0}(\mathcal{S}ym)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is the mod
p
basic representation. Let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}C_{p}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
be the CF functor given by semisimplification. Then it is clear that
\Phi_{\varphi}(\mathbf{1}_{S_{n}})=\mathbf{1}_{S_{n-p}}
. By Corollary
5.7.2
, this implies
e^{p}
defines an endomorphism of the mod
p
basic representation which is locally nilpotent but for which no power is zero. It follows that this representation is reducible and has an infinite composition series, which in particular reproves the reducibility result from
[
11
]
.

6.
OTI functors on Symmetric groups

In this section, we consider symmetric groups and show that OTI functors correspond to taking fixed points on the underlying set of the permutation modules
M^{\lambda}
. We introduce a stable range of partitions for which OTI functors are an equivalence on the corresponding subcategories of permutations modules. This result was essentially proven in
[
27
]
and
[
24
]
, but we recast the proof in terms of OTI functors. We will extend this equivalence to certain abelian subcategories of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
in Section
8
.

Throughout this section, we will fix a prime power
p^{r}>1
and consider natural numbers
n
such that
n\geq p^{r}
. We will view
S_{p^{r}}
embedded in
S_{n}
as the subgroup fixing
\{1,\dots,n-p^{r}\}
pointwise, and consider the subgroup
S_{n-p^{r}}\subseteq S_{n}
which fixes
\{n-p^{r}+1,\dots,n-1,n\}
pointwise. Let
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
be a transitive subgroup and fix a CF functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
.
We will investigate the OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}
(see Example
5.5.3
),

\Phi_{\varphi}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Res}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\xrightarrow{\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\varphi}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.

6.1.
Partitions and permutation modules in the stable range

We follow the notations and conventions used in
[
32
]
for partitions, the dominance order, Young diagrams, and tableaux. In particular we write
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{\ell(\lambda}))
for a partition
\lambda
, where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}>0
. If
\lambda
is a partition of
n
such that
\lambda_{i}\geq k
, then we write
\lambda-ke_{i}
for the unique partition of
n-k
with parts
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{i}-k,\lambda_{i+1},\ldots,\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}
. If
\lambda,\mu
are partitions, then the dominance order on partitions says that
\lambda\geq\mu
if for all
i
we have

\sum\limits_{j\leq i}\lambda_{j}\geq\sum\limits_{j\leq i}\mu_{j}.

For a partition
\lambda\vdash n
, we will write
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}:=S_{n}/S_{\lambda}
, where
S_{\lambda}:=S_{\lambda_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}
. The permutation module
\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}]
associated to
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
will be written as
M^{\lambda}
, and the Young module as
Y^{\lambda}
. We further write
S^{\lambda}
for the Specht module corresponding to
\lambda
, and
D^{\lambda}
for the irreducible
S_{n}
-module corresponding to a
p
-regular partition
\lambda
.

Definition 6.1.1
.

Let
\lambda\vdash n
. We say that

(1)

\lambda
is
p^{r}
-quasistable
if
\lambda_{1}\geq p^{r}
and
\lambda_{2}<p^{r}
;

(2)

\lambda
is
p^{r}
-stable
if both

(a)

\sum\limits_{i=2}^{\ell(\lambda)}\lambda_{j}<p^{r}
, and

(b)

if
n<3p^{r}
, then
\lambda_{1}\geq\frac{n+p^{r}}{2}
;

(3)

\lambda
is
p^{r}
-truncated
if
\lambda=\mu-p^{r}e_{1}
for some
p^{r}
-stable partition
\mu\vdash(n+p^{r})
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

For
n\geq 3p^{r}
, any
p^{r}
-stable partition
\lambda\vdash n
satisfies
\lambda_{1}>n-p^{r}\geq\frac{n+p^{r}}{2}
. In this case
p^{r}
-stable is equivalent to
p^{r}
-truncated.

The following lemma is straightforward, but will be important later.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

The subsets of
p^{r}
-stable and
p^{r}
-truncated partitions of
n
are upwards closed with respect to the dominance order of partitions.

Proposition 6.1.4
.

For
\lambda\vdash n
, the following holds:

(1)

The inclusion
(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda})^{H}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
induces an isomorphism

\Phi_{\varphi}(\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}])\cong\Bbbk[(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda})^{H}]\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1}).

(2)

The
H
-fixed points on
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
decompose, as an
S_{n-p^{r}}
-set, as

(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda})^{H}\cong\coprod\limits_{\lambda_{i}\geq p^{r}}\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda-p^{r}e_{i}}.

In particular, we have an isomorphism

\Phi_{\varphi}(M^{\lambda})\cong\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda_{i}\geq p^{r}}M^{\lambda-p^{r}e_{i}}\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1}).

(3)

(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda})^{H}
has exactly one
S_{n-p^{r}}
-orbit if and only if
\lambda
is
p^{r}
-quasistable.

Proof.

For (1),
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
decomposes into finitely many
H
-orbits
\mathcal{O}_{i}
. We thus have an isomorphism of
H
-modules

\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}]\cong\bigoplus_{i}\Bbbk[\mathcal{O}_{i}].

By the definition of a CF functor, we are done if we can show that
\varphi
annihilates
\Bbbk[\mathcal{O}_{i}]
if and only if
\mathcal{O}_{i}
is not a singleton. Since these are permutation modules, it is equivalent to show that the stabilizer of any
t\in\mathcal{O}_{i}
is either all of
H
or is not transitive. However the stabilizer of
t
in
S_{n}
is a conjugate
S^{\sigma}_{\lambda}:=\sigma S_{\lambda}\sigma^{-1}
of
S_{\lambda}
in
S_{n}
. We have
H\cap S_{\lambda}^{\sigma}\subseteq S_{p^{r}}\cap S^{\sigma}_{\lambda}
, and the latter is transitive if and only if
S_{p^{r}}\cap S_{\lambda}^{\sigma}=S_{p^{r}}
, or equivalently
H\cap S_{\lambda}^{\sigma}=H
.

To prove (2), we use the explicit description of
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
as fillings of the Young diagram of
\lambda
with the set
\{1,2..,n\}
such that the entries in each row are increasing. From this description, we see that if
t\in\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}
is fixed by
H
, then by transitivity of
H
, the labels
n,n-1,\ldots,n-p^{r}+1
must be contained in the same row of
t
, and hence this row must have at least
p^{r}
boxes. Removing these boxes from
\lambda
gives a tableau of shape
\lambda-p^{r}e_{i}
for some
i
. It is easy to check that this procedure yields a bijection.

Finally, part (3) is clear from (2).
∎

Remark 6.1.5
.

By Proposition
6.1.4
,
\Phi_{\varphi}(M^{\lambda})
is a direct sum of permutation modules
M^{\mu}
where each
\mu
is obtained by removing
p^{r}
boxes from some row of the Young diagram of
\lambda
. In light of this, the condition that
\lambda
is
p^{r}
-quasistable is equivalent to the Young diagram of
\lambda
having exactly one row where
p^{r}
boxes can be removed.

6.2.
Periodic equivalence for permutation modules

Write
\operatorname{Perm}_{n}
(resp.
\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r},n}
,
\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r},n}
) for the full, additive subcategory of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
containing permutation modules
M^{\lambda}
for all (resp.
p^{r}
-stable,
p^{r}
-truncated) partitions
\lambda\vdash n
. Similarly write
\operatorname{Young}_{n}
(resp.
\operatorname{Young}_{p^{r},n}^{\operatorname{st}}
,
\operatorname{Young}_{p^{r},n}^{\operatorname{tr}}
) for the full, additive subcategories generated by the corresponding Young modules. Note that the subcategories
\operatorname{Young}_{p^{r},n}^{(-)}
