are equivalent to the idempotent completions of the categories
\operatorname{Perm}_{p^{r},n}^{(-)}
.

In the remainder of this section, we suppose that
\mathscr{A}
is a locally finite abelian category.
Given a simple object
L
in
\mathscr{A}
, write
\langle L\rangle
for the smallest full, abelian, replete subcategory containing
L
. It is easy to see that
\langle L\rangle\simeq\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
, and we will abuse this identification going forward, simply writing vector spaces for objects in this subcategory. Similarly, if
\mathscr{C}
is another abelian category such that
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
exists, we will identify the subcategory
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\langle L\rangle
with
\mathscr{C}
.

The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 6.2.1
.

Suppose
\varphi(\mathbf{1})
is simple. Then
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{st}}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}.

Passing to idempotent completions, we obtain:

Corollary 6.2.2
.

Suppose
\varphi(\mathbf{1})
is simple.
Then
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Young}^{\operatorname{st}}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Young}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}.

Remark 6.2.3
.

A statement equivalent to Theorem
6.2.1
can be found in
[
27
]
, Theorem 3.3, albeit in a different language (see also
[
39
]
). A (slightly weaker) formulation which is more in the spirit of our work appears in
[
24
]
.

We will denote the category of signed Young modules by
\operatorname{sgnYoung}_{n}:=\operatorname{Young}_{n}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
.
The proofs of this section can be readily adapted to prove the following Corollary.

Corollary 6.2.4
.

Suppose
\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is simple. Then
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{sgnYoung}^{\operatorname{st}}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{sgnYoung}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}.

For the proof of Theorem
6.2.1
, we begin with a lemma.

Lemma 6.2.5
.

Let
\lambda,\mu\vdash n
be
p^{r}
-stable. Then we have an isomorphism of
S_{n}
-sets

\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu}\cong\coprod_{i}\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}},

where each
\nu_{i}
that appears is
p^{r}
-quasistable.

Proof.

By the Mackey formula, the
\nu_{i}
that appear correspond to Young subgroups
S_{\lambda}\cap S_{\mu}^{g}
for some
g\in S_{n}
.
Hence it suffices to show that
|S_{\lambda_{i}}\cap S^{g}_{\lambda_{j}}|\geq p^{r}
if and only if
i=j=1
.
Suppose
(i,j)\neq(1,1)
.
Then

|S_{\lambda_{i}}\cap S^{g}_{\lambda_{j}}|\leq\min\{|S_{\lambda_{i}}|,|S^{g}_{\lambda_{j}}|\}<p^{r}
follows immediately by the stability assumption.

It remains to show
|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cap S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|\geq p^{r}
. This follows from applying the inclusion-exclusion principle to the union of
S_{\lambda_{1}}
and
S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}
.
By the stability assumptions,

\displaystyle n\geq|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cup S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|

\displaystyle=|S_{\lambda_{1}}|+|S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|-|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cap S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|

\displaystyle\geq 2\cdot\left(\frac{n+p^{r}}{2}\right)-|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cap S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|

\displaystyle=n+p^{r}-|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cap S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|,

hence
|S_{\lambda_{1}}\cap S^{g}_{\mu_{1}}|\geq p^{r}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem 6.2.1.

By Proposition
6.1.4
(2), the essential image of the functor

\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{st}}_{n,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{\varphi}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n-p^{r}}.

is exactly
\operatorname{Perm}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{n-p^{r},p^{r}}
. Hence it suffices to show that if
\lambda,\mu
are
p^{r}
-stable, then
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n}}(M^{\lambda},M^{\mu})\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n-p^{r}}}(\Phi_{\varphi}(M^{\lambda}),\Phi_{\varphi}(M^{\mu})).

Proposition
6.1.4
(1) shows this is equivalent to

\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n}}(\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}],\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n-p^{r}}}(\Bbbk[(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda})^{H}],\Bbbk[(\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu})^{H}]).

Permutation modules are self-dual, so we need to check that

\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n}}(\mathbf{1},\Bbbk[\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu}])\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{n-p^{r}}}(\mathbf{1},\Bbbk[(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu})^{H}]).

This follows because the inclusion
(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu})^{H}\subseteq(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu})
induces a bijection of orbits

S_{n-p^{r}}\backslash(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu})^{H}\xrightarrow{\sim}S_{n}\backslash(\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu}).

Indeed, Lemma
6.2.5
shows that
\operatorname{Tab}^{\lambda}\times\operatorname{Tab}^{\mu}
decomposes as a disjoint union
\coprod_{i}\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}}
for
p^{r}
-quasistable partitions
\nu_{i}
. By Proposition
6.1.4
(3), each inclusion
(\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}})^{H}\subseteq\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}}
induces a bijection of orbits
S_{n-p^{r}}\backslash(\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}})^{H}\xrightarrow{\sim}S_{n}\backslash\operatorname{Tab}^{\nu_{i}}
.
∎

7.
OTI on Polynomial functors

One of the basic examples of a category admitting an action of the degenerate Heisenberg algebra is the category of strict polynomial functors. In this section we recall this example (Section
7.1
), and study the OTI functor in this setting (Section
7.2
). This example is related to representations of symmetric groups via Schur–Weyl duality, and in Section
7.3
we prove the compatibility of Schur–Weyl duality with the OTI functor. This will be used in Section
8
to prove periodicity results for symmetric groups.

7.1.
Polynomial functors

The following is due to Friedlander and Suslin
[
20
]
.

Definition 7.1.1
.

The category
\mathcal{P}ol
of strict polynomial functors consists of functors
M:\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\to\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
,
such that for any
V,W\in\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
:

(1)

The morphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V,W)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M(V),M(W))
is polynomial for all
V,W
.

(2)

The degree of the map
\operatorname{End}(V)\to\operatorname{End}(M(V))
is uniformly bounded over all
V\in\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
.

Morphisms in
\mathcal{P}ol
are given by natural transformations.
We say that
M\in\mathcal{P}ol
has
degree

d\in\mathbb{N}
if for any vector spaces
V,W
, the morphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(V,W)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\Bbbk}(M(V),M(W)),

is homogeneous of degree
d
. We write
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
for the full subcategory of
\mathcal{P}ol
of degree
d
polynomial functors.

Example 7.1.2
.

The following are objects in
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
:

(1)

The
d
-th tensor power functor
V\mapsto\bigotimes^{d}V
.

(2)

The
d
-th symmetric power functor
V\mapsto S^{d}(V)
.

(3)

The
d
-th divided power functor
V\mapsto\Gamma^{d}(V)\coloneqq(\bigotimes^{d}V)^{S_{d}}
.

(4)

The
d
-th exterior power functor
V\mapsto\Lambda^{d}(V)
.

(5)

Any Schur functor corresponding to a partition of
d
.

The category
\mathcal{P}ol
is
\Bbbk
-linear and abelian, and has a natural decomposition:

\mathcal{P}ol=\bigoplus\limits_{d\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}ol_{d}.

There is a monoidal product on
\mathcal{P}ol
given by

(M\otimes N)(V):=M(V)\otimes N(V).

Observe that
\otimes
is an exact bifunctor on
\mathcal{P}ol
, and
\otimes:\mathcal{P}ol_{d_{1}}\times\mathcal{P}ol_{d_{2}}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d_{1}+d_{2}}
.

Recall that a rational representation
M\in\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}GL(V)
is polynomial of degree
d
if its matrix coefficients are homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
.

Lemma 7.1.3
.

Write
\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL(V)
for the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}GL(V)
consisting of polynomial representations of degree
d
.

(1)

[
20
]
Suppose that
\operatorname{dim}V\geq d
. Then the functor:

M\mapsto M(V)

gives an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL(V)
.

(2)

[
29
]
If
n>d
, then the functor
R_{0}:\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL(\Bbbk^{n})\to\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL(\Bbbk^{n-1})
,

M\mapsto M^{GL_{1}},

given by restriction to
GL_{n-1}\times GL_{1}
and taking
GL_{1}
invariants, is an equivalence such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Pol}_{d}GL_{n-1}}

We recall that
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
is a highest weight category
[
13
]
, where the standard objects are Weyl functors
\Delta_{\lambda}
and costandard objects are Schur functors
\nabla_{\lambda}
.
The tilting objects for this highest weight structure are summands of tensor products
\Lambda^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}}
.

For a partition
\lambda
of
d
, we write
L_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
for the simple object
and
T_{\lambda}\in\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
for the indecomposable tilting object of highest weight
\lambda
.
We write
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d}
for the full additive subcategory of
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
consisting of tilting objects.

We also recall that
\mathcal{P}ol
admits a categorical Heisenberg action in the sense of Section
5.2
, see
[
30
]
. Here the functor
F
is given by

F(M)(V):=M(V)\otimes V,

while
E
is given by

E(M)(V):=M(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{1}.

In the above, we consider the embedding
GL(\Bbbk)\subseteq GL(V\oplus\Bbbk)
induced by the natural map
\Bbbk\to V\oplus\Bbbk
, and we take the eigenspace of weight
1
with respect to this copy of
GL(\Bbbk)\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Observe that
F
increases the degree of a polynomial functor by one, while
E
decreases the degree by one.

The natural transformation
T:E^{2}\to E^{2}
is induced by the natural map
V\oplus\Bbbk\oplus\Bbbk\to V\oplus\Bbbk\oplus\Bbbk
which permutes the last two entries.

7.2.
OTI functors

Choose a CF functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
for
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
. In this section we will study the corresponding OTI functor

\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\varphi}(M)(V)

\displaystyle=\varphi(E^{p^{r}}(M)(V))=\varphi(M(V\oplus\Bbbk^{p^{r}})^{\overbrace{\scriptsize\scriptstyle 1,1,\dots,1}^{p^{r}}}),

where
we are taking the
(1,\ldots,1)
weight space with respect to the action of
\underbrace{\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{p^{r}\text{copies}}
.

Remark 7.2.1
.

The OTI functor induced by
F
instead of
E
is given by

M\mapsto(V\mapsto M(V)\otimes V^{(r)})\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1}),

as in Example
5.5.5
.
Notice that this functor only depends on
\varphi(\mathbf{1})
, which is not the case for the functor
\Phi_{\varphi}
above.

Example 7.2.2
.

We evaluate the OTI functor on some basic examples of polynomial functors. In the following we assume that
d\geq p^{r}
.

(1)

First, we show that
\Phi_{\varphi}(\otimes^{d})=0
. Set
M=\otimes^{d}
. Then we have that

(7.2.3)

\displaystyle E^{p^{r}}(M)(V)\cong\operatorname{Ind}^{S_{d}}_{S_{d-p^{r}}}(V^{\otimes(d-p^{r})}).

Note that the
S_{d}
-module structure in
7.2.3
comes from the natural action of
S_{d}
on
M
. On the other hand, there is also an
S_{p^{r}}
-action on
E^{p^{r}}
,
and via (
7.2.3
) this corresponds to right multiplication by
S_{p^{r}}
(embedded in
S_{d}
as the permutations of
d-p^{r}+1,\ldots,d
). More precisely, for
g\in S_{d}
,
\xi\in V^{\otimes(d-p^{r})}
and
h\in S_{p^{r}}
,

(g\otimes\xi)\cdot h=gh\otimes\xi.

Note this action is free (indeed if
K\times H\leq G
then right multiplication by
H
on

\operatorname{Ind}_{K}^{G}(U)
is free for any
K
-module
U
).
Therefore the CF functor
\varphi
must kill
E^{p^{r}}(M)(V)
.

(2)

For symmetric powers
S^{d}
, we show that
\Phi_{\varphi}(S^{d})=S^{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1})
. We know

\displaystyle E(S^{d})(V)=S^{d}(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{1}\cong\bigoplus_{a+b=d}S^{a}(V)\otimes S^{b}(\Bbbk)^{1}

Observe that
S^{b}(\Bbbk)^{1}=0
unless
b=1
. Hence we have
E(S^{d})=S^{d-1}
, and iterating we get that
E^{p^{r}}(S^{d})=S^{d-p^{r}}
. Since
S_{p^{r}}
acts trivially, the result follows.

(3)

Similarly, we have that
\Phi_{\varphi}(\Gamma^{d})=\Gamma^{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\varphi(\mathbf{1})
. Indeed, as in (2) we have
\Gamma^{d}(V\oplus\Bbbk)\cong\bigoplus_{a+b=d}\Gamma^{a}(V)\otimes\Gamma^{b}(\Bbbk)
and
\Gamma^{b}(\Bbbk)^{1}=0
unless
b=1
. Therefore
E^{p^{r}}(\Gamma^{d})=\Gamma^{d-p^{r}}
, and
S_{p^{r}}
acts trivially.

(4)

Finally,
\Phi_{\varphi}(\Lambda^{d})=\Lambda^{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
. Indeed,
\Lambda
satisfies the same compatibility with
\oplus,\otimes
as in (2) and (3), and
\Lambda^{b}(\Bbbk)=0
for
b>1
. It follows that
E^{p^{r}}(\Lambda^{d})=\Lambda^{d-p^{r}}
, and
S_{p^{r}}
acts by the sign representation.

For the remainder of Section
7
, we assume that
\mathscr{A}
is a locally finite abelian category.
Recall that given a simple object
L
in
\mathscr{A}
, we identify the subcategory
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\langle L\rangle
of
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
with
\mathscr{C}
(see Section
6.2
).

Lemma 7.2.4
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
satisfies

\Phi_{\varphi}(\operatorname{Tilt}_{d})\subseteq\operatorname{Tilt}_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\langle\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})\rangle.

Thus if
\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is simple,
\Phi_{\varphi}
induces a functor
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d}\to\operatorname{Tilt}_{d-p^{r}}
, which is independent of the choice of
\varphi
.

Proof.

Since
\Phi_{\varphi}
is additive and any tilting object in
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
is a summand of a tensor power of the form
\Lambda^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}}
, the result follows if we prove

(7.2.5)

\Phi_{\varphi}(\Lambda^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}})\cong\left(\bigoplus_{k_{i}\geq p^{r}}\Lambda^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{i}-p^{r}}\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}}\right)\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H}).

First, we show that
E
is a derivation, i.e.
E(M\otimes N)\cong(E(M)\otimes N)\oplus(M\otimes E(N))
. Indeed, observe that

\displaystyle E(M\otimes N)(V)\cong\left(M(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{1}\otimes N(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{0}\right)\oplus\left(M(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{0}\otimes N(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{1}\right),

and by part (2) of Lemma
7.1.3
, we have
M(V\oplus\Bbbk)^{0}\cong M(V)
.

Next, consider
M=M_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes M_{j}
. By the derivation property we have

\displaystyle E^{n}(M)\cong\bigoplus_{n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j}=n}(E^{n_{1}}(M_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes E^{n_{j}}(M_{j}))^{\binom{n}{n_{1},...,n_{j}}}.

Specialising to the case
M=\Lambda^{k_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}}
and
n=p^{r}
, summands in
E^{p^{r}}(M)
look like

(\Lambda^{k_{1}-n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}-n_{j}})^{\binom{p^{r}}{n_{1},...,n_{j}}}.

Letting
S_{n_{i}}
act by sign on
\Lambda^{k_{i}-n_{i}}
, we have that this is isomorphic to

(7.2.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{n_{j}}}^{S_{p^{r}}}(\Lambda^{k_{1}-n_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{k_{j}-n_{j}}).

The terms where
n_{i}\neq p^{r}
for all
i
will be killed by
\varphi
, because
S_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{n_{j}}
is a non-transitive subgroup. On the remaining terms,
S_{p^{r}}
acts by sign, so we obtain (
7.2.5
).
∎

7.3.
Schur–Weyl duality

We consider the Schur–Weyl duality functor

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\operatorname{Rep}S_{d},\hskip 10.00002ptM\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{P}ol_{d}}(\otimes^{d},M).

We recommend
[
26
]
for basic properties of Schur–Weyl duality, including the following.
The functor
\mathcal{F}
is exact and admits a right adjoint which we denote by
\mathcal{G}
. Explicitly, we have
\mathcal{G}(N):V\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{S_{d}}(V^{\otimes d},N)
.
Note that
\mathcal{F}\mathcal{G}\cong\operatorname{id}
.

Let
\lambda^{t}
denote the dual of the partition
\lambda
.
On tilting objects we have
\mathcal{F}(T_{\lambda})\cong Y^{\lambda^{t}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
, and
\mathcal{F}
is fully faithful on
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d}
.
We also have that
\mathcal{F}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{j}})\cong M^{\lambda^{t}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
, and
\mathcal{F}
maps simple objects to simple objects (or zero).

It follows that
\mathcal{F}
induces an equivalence

(7.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}:\operatorname{Tilt}_{d}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{sgnYoung}_{d}.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

The OTI functor commutes with Schur–Weyl duality, i.e. the following diagram commutes up to natural isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}ol_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}S_{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}S_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}S_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{p^{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\boxtimes\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{p^{r}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}\boxtimes\varphi}

In particular, we have a natural isomorphism
(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes 1)\Phi_{\varphi}\simeq\Phi_{\varphi}\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

Recall that the categories
\mathcal{P}ol
and
\mathcal{S}ym=\bigoplus\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
carry categorical Heisenberg actions. In
[
30
]
it is shown that
\mathcal{F}
is a morphism of categorical actions, from which it easily follows that the left square commutes.
On the other hand, because
\mathcal{F}
is exact, by Lemma
2.2.1
(2) we have
(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes 1)(1\boxtimes\varphi)\cong(1\boxtimes\varphi)(\mathcal{F}\boxtimes 1)
, and hence the right square commutes as well.
∎

Write
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
(resp.
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}
) for the full additive subcategory of
\operatorname{Tilt}_{d}
generated by indecomposable tilting modules
T(\lambda)
, where
\lambda^{t}
is
p^{r}
-stable (resp.
p^{r}
-truncated).

Proposition 7.3.3
.

If
\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is simple,

\Phi_{\varphi}
induces an equivalence

\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}.

Proof.

By (
7.3.1
),
\mathcal{F}
induces equivalences

\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{sgnYoung}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}},\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{sgnYoung}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}.

Hence by Lemma
7.3.2
, the result follows from Corollary
6.2.4
and Lemma
7.2.4
.
∎

We write
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}
) for the Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
generated by the simple objects
L_{\lambda}
, where
\lambda^{t}
is
p^{r}
-stable (resp.
p^{r}
-truncated). By
[
7
, Theorem 3.17]
and Lemma
6.1.3
, these define highest weight subcategories of
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
.

Lemma 7.3.4
.

We have
E^{p^{r}}(\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}})\subseteq\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}
. Hence
\Phi_{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}})\subseteq\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
.

Proof.

Recall that
E
is exact and tilting objects in
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
generate
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
as an abelian category.
Therefore it suffices to show that
E^{p^{r}}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{j}})
lies in
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}
whenever
\lambda
is
p^{r}
-stable. By (
7.2.6
), we see that
E^{p^{r}}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{j}})
is a direct sum of tiltings
T_{\mu^{t}}
, where
\mu\leq\lambda-p^{r}e_{1}
. Since
\lambda-p^{r}e_{1}
is
p^{r}
-truncated by definition, we obtain that
\mu
must also be
p^{r}
-truncated.
∎

8.
Periodicity

In this section, we prove Theorems
A
and
C
. We first prove analogous equivalences for polynomial functors, exploiting the highest weight structure. The equivalence for representations of symmetric groups is deduced using Schur–Weyl duality. Theorem
A
is proven by extending the equivalence on tiltings (Proposition
7.3.3
). We use the exactness property (Lemma
3.3.3
) for CF functors given by semisimplification with respect to (generic) shifted cyclic subgroups. For more general CF functors, we deduce the result using a technical lemma on highest weight categories.

8.1.
Highest weight categories

We begin by recalling some background on highest weight categories, for more detail see
[
35
, Section 3]
(together with
[
36
, Remark 8.1.7]
), or more generally
[
36
, Section 8.1]
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Lambda)
be a highest weight category. Note that we always assume the poset
\Lambda
is finite.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we will denote the corresponding irreducible, standard and costandard object by
L_{\lambda}
,
\Delta_{\lambda}
and
\nabla_{\lambda}
, respectively.
If
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
, we write
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
for the Serre subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
generated by the irreducibles
L_{\mu}
with
\mu\in\Lambda_{0}
.

Similarly, we
write
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\Lambda_{0}})
for the full subcategory of objects

X
in
\mathscr{C}
that admit a finite filtration

0=X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq X_{t}=X
such that each
factor
X_{i}/X_{i-1}
is isomorphic to some
\Delta_{\mu}
with
\mu\in\Lambda_{0}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta):=\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\Lambda})
.

Now fix a maximal element
\lambda\in\Lambda
and set
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda\}
.
Recall that
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is again a highest weight category, with standard objects
\{\Delta_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\Lambda_{0}\}
and costandard objects
\{\nabla_{\mu}\mid\mu\in\Lambda_{0}\}
. The inclusion functor
i_{*}:\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}\to\mathscr{C}
has a left (resp., right) adjoint sending an object of
\mathscr{C}
to the largest quotient (resp., subobject) that belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
This results in a recollement of abelian catetories,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}

with
\mathscr{C}_{\lambda}:=\mathscr{C}_{\{\lambda\}}
equivalent to
\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}
. Note that the left (resp., right) adjoint of
j^{*}
sends
\Bbbk
to the projective (resp., injective) object
\Delta_{\lambda}
(resp.,
\nabla_{\lambda}
).

Restriction to
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
gives a diagram of exact functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\Lambda_{0}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\lambda})\simeq\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}

such that for every
M
in
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
, the unit and counit fit into a short exact sequence

0\to j_{!}j^{*}M\to M\to i_{*}i^{*}M\to 0.

Of course, dual statements hold for
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\nabla)
.

8.2.
Additive functors on highest weight categories

In this section, we will consider an additive functor
\phi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
from a highest weight category
(\mathscr{C},\Lambda)
to an abelian category
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
.
Note that highest weight categories have finite global dimension. From
\phi
we will derive three triangulated functors
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
on the bounded derived categories:

•

The
tilting derived functor

\tilde{\phi}
is the composition

\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathbf{K}^{b}(\operatorname{Tilt}(\mathscr{C}))\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathbf{K}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}),

where
\mathbf{K}^{b}(-)
denotes the bounded homotopy category. To compute
\tilde{\phi}(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{C}
, apply
\phi
degreewise to a finite complex of tilting modules quasi-isomorphic to
M
.

•

The
left derived functor

\mathbf{L}\phi
is defined as the left Kan extension of

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
along
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})
.
In particular,
\mathbf{L}\phi
is the composition

\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathbf{K}^{b}(\operatorname{proj}\nolimits(\mathscr{C}))\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathbf{K}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}).

To compute
\mathbf{L}\phi(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{C}
, replace
M
by a projective resolution and apply
\phi
degreewise.

•

The
right derived functor

\mathbf{R}\phi
is the right Kan extension of

\mathscr{C}\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
along
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})
.
In particular,
\mathbf{R}\phi
is the composition

\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathbf{K}^{b}(\operatorname{inj}\nolimits(\mathscr{C}))\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathbf{K}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime}).

To compute
\mathbf{R}\phi(M)
for
M\in\mathscr{C}
, replace
M
by an injective resolution and apply
\phi
degreewise.

Note that we get natural transformations
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\phi
of functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
as usual, where we view
\phi
as a functor
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
via composition with the embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
.
Since objects in
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
have right resolutions by tilting objects, we furthermore have a natural transformation
\phi\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}
of functors
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
.
Dually, there is a natural transformation
\tilde{\phi}\Rightarrow\phi
of functors
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\nabla)\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
.

Lemma 8.2.1
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\Lambda)
be a highest weight category and
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
an abelian category.
Let
\lambda\in\Lambda
be maximal and set
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda\setminus\{\lambda\}
.
Consider an additive functor
\phi:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and suppose

(1)

\phi
is exact when restricted to
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
,

(2)

the natural maps
\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})\rightarrow\tilde{\phi}(\Delta_{\lambda})
and
\tilde{\phi}(\nabla_{\lambda})\rightarrow\phi(\nabla_{\lambda})
are isomorphisms.

Then the functors
\mathbf{L}\phi\simeq\tilde{\phi}\simeq\mathbf{R}\phi:\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
are naturally isomorphic and
the restriction
\tilde{\phi}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
is a direct summand of
\phi
.

Proof.

Consider the following diagram of exact functors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\Lambda_{0}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{vec}_{\Bbbk}\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}}

We investigate the sequence of natural transformations
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}
on
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
.
First note that
(\mathbf{L}\phi)j_{!}\Rightarrow\phi j_{!}
and
\phi j_{!}\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}j_{!}
are both natural isomorphisms. Indeed, it suffices to check this on
j_{!}(\Bbbk)=\Delta_{\lambda}
.
Since
\Delta_{\lambda}
is projective in
\mathscr{C}
, clearly
\mathbf{L}\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})\rightarrow\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})
is an isomorphism, whereas
\phi(\Delta_{\lambda})\rightarrow\tilde{\phi}(\Delta_{\lambda})
is an isomorphism by assumption (2).

Next, we turn to the natural transformations
(\mathbf{L}\phi)i_{*}\Rightarrow\phi i_{*}
and
\phi i_{*}\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}i_{*}
.
The latter is an isomorphism, since
\phi i_{*}
and
\tilde{\phi}i_{*}
are both exact functors which agree on tilting objects in
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
To compute
(\mathbf{L}\phi)i_{*}
, let
M\in\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\Lambda_{0}})
and write down a projective resolution
P_{\bullet}\twoheadrightarrow i_{*}M
in
\mathscr{C}
.
Since projectives are in
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
, we get a short exact sequence of complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{!}j^{*}P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

In fact, the rows are exact as well, since all syzygys of
P_{\bullet}\twoheadrightarrow i_{*}M
remain in
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
by
[
38
, Lemma 2.1.1]
, and
i_{*}i^{*}
and
j_{!}j^{*}
are exact functors there. Since the top row is a long exact sequence of projectives, it is null homotopic, hence
P_{\bullet}\to i_{*}i^{*}P_{\bullet}
is a homotopy equivalence.
We can now compute

\mathbf{L}\phi(i_{*}M)=\phi(P_{\bullet})\simeq\phi(i_{*}i^{*}P_{\bullet})=\phi(i_{*}M),

where the last equality follows because
\phi
is exact on the subcategory
\mathscr{C}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
We conclude that
(\mathbf{L}\phi)i_{*}\Rightarrow\phi i_{*}
is an isomorphism as well.

The composition
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}
applied to the exact sequence

0\to j_{!}j^{*}M\to M\to i_{*}i^{*}M\to 0

with
M
in
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)

gives us a morphism of triangles in
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}\phi(j_{!}j^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}\phi(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{L}\phi(i_{*}i^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}(j_{!}j^{*}M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\phi}(i_{*}i^{*}M)\ ,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

hence
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}
is a natural isomorphism on
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta)
.
In particular, the exact functors
\mathbf{L}\phi
and
\tilde{\phi}
are naturally isomorphic on
\operatorname{proj}\nolimits(\mathscr{C})
, so they are isomorphic on
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})
.

Dually, one can prove that the composition
\tilde{\phi}\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\phi
is an equivalence on
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\nabla)
, hence
\mathbf{R}\phi
and
\tilde{\phi}
are isomorphic on
\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})
.

Finally, we consider the composition
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\phi
of functors
\mathscr{C}\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
.
On tilting objects, it factors as the composition of the isomorphisms
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\tilde{\phi}
and
\tilde{\phi}\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\phi
. It follows that
\mathbf{L}\phi\Rightarrow\phi\Rightarrow\mathbf{R}\phi
is an isomorphism of exact functors on tilting objects, and hence an isomorphism on
\mathscr{C}
. We conclude that
\tilde{\phi}\simeq\mathbf{L}\phi\simeq\mathbf{R}\phi
is a direct summand of
\phi
.
∎

Remark 8.2.2
.

Recall that an exact functor on a highest weight category
\mathscr{C}
is entirely determined by its restriction to the subcategory of tilting objects in
\mathscr{C}
. In particular, if an exact functor
\phi_{1}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
and additive functor
\phi_{2}:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
agree on tilting objects, it follows that
\phi_{1}
agrees with the tilting derived functor
\tilde{\phi}_{2}:\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C})\to\mathbf{D}^{b}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
of
\phi_{2}
.
We will use this freely throughout the remainder of Section
8
, and will often identify
\tilde{\phi}_{2}
with its (co)restriction
\phi_{1}
.

8.3.
The OTI functor associated to a shifted cyclic subgroup

Let
A\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
denote a transitive elementary abelian
p
-group of order
p^{r}
. In other words,
A\cong C_{p}^{r}
and acts transitively on
\{1,\dots,p^{r}\}
. Such a subgroup is unique up to conjugacy, and may be realised for instance by letting
C_{p}^{r}
act by left multiplication on itself.

We will fix
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})\in\Bbbk^{n}
with
\mathbf{a}\neq 0
, and set
z_{\mathbf{a}}:=a_{1}x_{1}+\dots+a_{n}x_{n}\in\Bbbk A
, where
1-x_{1},\dots,1-x_{r}
are generators for
A
. Then the subalgebra
R_{\mathbf{a}}\subseteq\Bbbk A
generated by
z_{\mathbf{a}}
is a shifted cyclic subgroup (see Section
4.3
).
We now impose the following genericity condition on
\mathbf{a}
: we assume that
R_{\mathbf{a}}
does not lie in a group algebra
\Bbbk A^{\prime}
for any proper subgroup
A^{\prime}\subset A
. This will hold, for example, if
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Definition 8.3.1
.

We will consider the composition

\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}A\to R_{\mathbf{a}}\operatorname{--}\operatorname{mod}\to\operatorname{Ver}_{p},

where the first functor is restriction along
R_{\mathbf{a}}\subseteq\Bbbk A
and the second functor is defined as in Equations (
3.2.1
) and (
3.3.1
).

We caution that our genericity assumption implies that
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}
is
not
a monoidal functor.

Lemma 8.3.2
.

If
\mathbf{a}
is generic, then
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}
is a CF functor for
A\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to check that
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}
vanishes on induced modules
\operatorname{Ind}_{A^{\prime}}^{A}(M)
for proper subgroups
A^{\prime}\subset A
. This is equivalent to showing that
\operatorname{Ind}_{A^{\prime}}^{A}(M)
is projective over
R_{\mathbf{a}}
. In the language of support theory, this means we want to show that
\mathbf{a}
does not lie in the support of
\operatorname{Ind}_{A^{\prime}}^{A}(M)
. However this follows from
[
19
, Prop. 8.2.4]
.
∎

We write

\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}:=\Phi_{\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}}:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}

for the OTI functor on polynomial functors corresponding to
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}
. In Section
7.3
we introduced the highest weight subcategories
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
and
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d,p^{r}}
of
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}
, and we recall that
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}})\subseteq\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
by Lemma
7.3.4
.

Theorem 8.3.3
.

The functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Ver}_{p}
corestricts to an equivalence
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}
of highest weight categories.

Proof.

By Proposition
7.3.3
,
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
defines an equivalence between the corresponding categories of tilting modules (because
\operatorname{sgn}_{A}\cong\mathbf{1}
). Recall that such an equivalence of tilting modules extends uniquely to an equivalence
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}
of highest weight categories (see
[
24
, Corollary 1.6]
, for instance). By Remark
8.2.2
,
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}
is the (co)restriction of the tilting derived functor of
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
.

We claim that if
\lambda^{t}
is
p^{r}
-stable, the natural maps
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda})\rightarrow\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda})
and
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda})\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda})
are isomorphisms.
Indeed, let

0\to\Delta_{\lambda}\to T_{0}\to T_{1}\to\cdots\to T_{r}\to 0,

be a resolution of
\Delta_{\lambda}
by tilting objects. By induction on
r
, repeatedly applying Lemma
3.3.3
shows that
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda})\to\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(T_{\bullet})
is exact and that
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda})\to\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda})
is an isomorphism.
A similar argument shows
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda})
.

By iteratively applying Lemma
8.2.1
, we find a natural (split) transformation
\tilde{\Phi}_{\mathbf{a}}\Rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
on
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
. Consider the full subcategory of objects in
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
where this is an isomorphism. It contains all tilting modules and by Lemma
3.3.3
it is closed under kernels of epimorphisms and cokernels of monomorphisms. Hence it must be all of
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

8.4.
Periodicity for polynomial functors

Now choose a subgroup
A\subseteq H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
, so that in particular
z_{\mathbf{a}}\in\Bbbk H
. For an
H
-module
V
, write

V^{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}:=\{m\in V:h\cdot m=\operatorname{sgn}_{H}(h)m\text{ for all }h\in H\},

and
V_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
for the
H
-module:

V_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}=\frac{V^{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}}{V^{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\cap\sum\limits_{h\in H}\operatorname{im}(h-\operatorname{sgn}_{H}(h))}.

Note that
V_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
is isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of
\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
, and we have a decomposition
V\cong V_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{\alpha}N_{\alpha}
, where each
N_{\alpha}
is an indecomposable
H
-module not isomorphic to
\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
. The following lemma is easy.

Lemma 8.4.1
.

The functor
(-)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
is a CF functor.

We will fix an exact and conservative forgetful functor
U:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
, which is constructed either by using Lemma
7.1.3
(1) or by choosing a
\Bbbk
-coalgebra
C
and an equivalence
\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\xrightarrow{\simeq}C\operatorname{--}\operatorname{comod}_{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H}
as in Section
2.2
.
We then have a commuting diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\boxtimes(-)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H.}

We will look at the OTI functor

\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}:\quad\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H

given by
M\mapsto(1\boxtimes(-)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}})(E^{p^{r}}M)
.
By Equations (
7.2.5
) and (
7.2.6
), the exact functor

\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}:\ \ \mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}}\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\xrightarrow{-\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\langle\operatorname{sgn}_{H}\rangle

coincides with
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
on the subcategory
\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
.

By Remark
8.2.2
and Lemma
7.2.4
, we may identify
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
with the tilting derived functor of
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
. In particular, we have a natural transformation
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\Rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
of functors
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{\{\lambda^{t}\mid\lambda\text{ is }p^{r}-\text{ stable}\}})\to\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
and a natural transformation
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}\Rightarrow\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
of functors
\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\nabla_{\{\lambda^{t}\mid\lambda\text{ is }p^{r}-\text{ stable}\}})\to\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H
.

Remark 8.4.2
.

Note that

U\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(M)\cong(UE^{p^{r}}(M))_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\cong\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(UE^{p^{r}}(M))_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}

is a direct summand of

U(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})(M)\cong\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}UE^{p^{r}}(M)\otimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}

for every
M\in\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}
.

Lemma 8.4.3
.

Suppose that
\lambda
is a
p^{r}
-stable partition.
Then the natural maps
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
and
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}\rightarrow\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})
are isomorphisms.

Proof.

We prove this for standard modules, with the statement for costandard modules being dual.
Since
U
is conservative, it suffices to show that
the natural transformation
U\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\Rightarrow U(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is an isomorphism when evaluated at
\Delta_{\lambda^{t}}
.

Since
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}
is an equivalence of highest weight categories (see Theorem
8.3.3
), it is clear that
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}=\Delta_{(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
.
Next, recall that we have a short exact sequence

0\to\Delta_{\lambda^{t}}\to\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}}\to M\to 0

with
M\in\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{<\lambda^{t}})
.
By Equation (
7.2.6
),
E^{p^{r}}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})
contains

(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}-p^{r}}\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}

as a direct summand, with complimentary summands of the form
N\boxtimes V
with
V\neq\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
indecomposable and
N\in\operatorname{Filt}\nolimits(\Delta_{<(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}})
.

We obtain the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}-p^{r}}\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{p^{r}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{p^{r}}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

After applying
U
, the upper horizontal arrow splits, and therefore the left vertical arrow splits as well.
In other words,
U(\Delta_{(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is a direct summand of

UE^{p^{r}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})
.
The following commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\Delta_{(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\Delta_{(\lambda-p^{r}e_{1})^{t}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})(\Lambda^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\Lambda^{\lambda_{\ell}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}

shows that the natural map
U\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow U\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
is a (split) epimorphism. The lemma now follows from Remark
8.4.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.4.4
.

The OTI functor
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
coincides with its tilting derived functor
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
8.4.3
, the natural maps
\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}
and
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}\rightarrow\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})
are isomorphisms for all
p^{r}
-stable partitions
\lambda
. By iteratively applying Lemma
8.2.1
, we find a natural transformation
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H}\Rightarrow\Phi_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}
which is a split monomorphism. Remark
8.4.2
then completes the proof.
∎

Now let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}(H)\to\mathscr{A}
be a CF functor, where
\mathscr{A}
is a locally finite abelian category. Consider the corresponding OTI functor

\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{P}ol_{d}\to\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}.

Recall that
\Phi_{\varphi}(\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}})\subseteq\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
by Lemma
7.3.4
.

Theorem 8.4.5
.

Suppose
\varphi
satisfies the following assumptions:

(1)

for all
H
-modules
V
we have
\varphi(V)=0
whenever
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(V)=0
, and

(2)

\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
is a simple object of
\mathscr{A}
.

Then the OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
coincides with
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
. In particular,
\Phi_{\varphi}
corestricts to an equivalence
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}
of highest weight categories, independent of the choice of
\varphi
.

We note that the semisimplification functor
\varphi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H)^{ss}
satisfies assumptions (1) and (2), as do the CF functors in Example
5.1.2
.
The conjectural CF functor constructed in
[
10
, Sec. 5.4]
does as well.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2.4
, the equivalence

\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
coincides with
\Phi_{\varphi}
on the subcategory
\operatorname{Tilt}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
.
In particular, we will identify
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})
with the tilting derived functor of
\Phi_{\varphi}
.
Now let
M\in\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
and choose a decomposition

UE^{p^{r}}M=(UE^{p^{r}}M)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\oplus V.

Applying
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}
to both sides and using Lemma
8.4.4
,
we find
U\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(M)\cong U\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(M)\oplus\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(V),

hence
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(V)=0
, which by
(1)
implies
\varphi(V)=0
.
Hence, using Lemma
8.4.4
once more,

U\Phi_{\varphi}(M)\cong\varphi((UE^{p^{r}}M)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\oplus V)\cong U(1\boxtimes\varphi)(E^{p^{r}}M)_{\operatorname{sgn}_{H}}\cong U(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}(M)\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})).

It is not hard to check that for
M=\Delta_{\lambda^{t}}
, the above isomorphism corresponds to the image under
U
of the natural map
\Phi_{\varphi}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H}))(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})
.
Since
U
detects isomorphisms,
\Phi_{\varphi}(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H}))(\Delta_{\lambda^{t}})
is an isomorphism for all
p^{r}
-stable partitions
\lambda
. Dually, the natural map
(\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H}))(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})\rightarrow\Phi_{\varphi}(\nabla_{\lambda^{t}})
is also an isomorphism for all such
\lambda
. By iteratively applying Lemma
8.2.1
, we find a natural transformation
\Phi_{\mathbf{a}}\boxtimes\varphi(\operatorname{sgn}_{H})\Rightarrow\Phi_{\varphi}
on
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
which is a split monomorphism. Since it must be an isomorphism after applying
U
, this natural transformation is an isomorphism.
∎

8.5.
Periodicity for symmetric groups

We would now like to translate Theorem
8.4.5
to
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{d}
. For this, define
\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}
(resp.
\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}
) to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d,p^{r}}
) on objects in the image of
\mathcal{F}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
. Let
\mathcal{K}_{d}^{\operatorname{st}}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}_{d}^{\operatorname{tr}}
) denote the Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}
) generated by simples
L_{\lambda}
where
\lambda^{t}
is
p
-singular. Let
\varphi
be as in Theorem
8.4.5
. Then Lemma
7.3.2
shows we have a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\ .}

Note that
\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
(resp.
\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d,p^{r}}
) is exactly the Serre subcategory of objects annihilated by
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}
). It is easy to see from Theorem
8.4.5
that
\Phi_{\varphi}:\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}
is an equivalence.

We will need the following lemma. Recall that a subcategory
\mathscr{A}\subset\mathscr{B}
is called
quotient reflective
if the inclusion
\mathscr{A}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{B}
has a left adjoint such that the unit of the adjunction is an epimorphism.

Lemma 8.5.1
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
be abelian categories and suppose
S:\mathscr{B}\to\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
is a Serre quotient functor. Let
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathscr{B}
be a quotient reflective abelian subcategory.
Write
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
for the essential image of
\mathscr{C}
under
S
. Then for every morphism
f:SX\to SY
in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
with
X,\,Y
in
\mathscr{C}
, there exist morphisms
g:Z\to X
and
h:Z\to Y
both in
\mathscr{C}
, so that
Sg
is an isomorphism and such that
f=Sh(Sg)^{-1}
.
In particular,
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
is an abelian subcategory and
S:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is a Serre quotient functor.

Proof.

Let
f:SX\to SY
be a morphism in
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
with
X,\,Y
in
\mathscr{C}
. Since
S
is a Serre quotient, there exists an object
Z
in
\mathscr{B}
and morphisms
g:Z\to X
and
h:Z\to Y
in
\mathscr{B}
, so that
Sg
is an isomorphism and such that
f=Sh(Sg)^{-1}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Since
X,\,Y
are in
\mathscr{C}
and the inclusion
i_{*}:\mathscr{C}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{B}
has a left adjoint
i^{*}
by assumption, we know that
g
and
h
factor via the unit morphism on
Z
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}i^{*}Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}

Since
Sg
is an isomorphism and
S\eta
is an epimorphism, it follows that
Sg^{\prime}
is an isomorphism with
f=Sh^{\prime}(Sg^{\prime})^{-1}
.
It follows immediately that
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is closed under kernels and cokernels in
\mathscr{B}^{\prime}
, hence is abelian, and that

S:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
is a Serre quotient functor.
∎

By Lemma
6.1.3
, we know that
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d,p^{r}}
and
\mathcal{P}ol^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}
are quotient reflective subcategories of
\mathcal{P}ol^{d}
, hence the following is an easy corollary of the lemma.

Corollary 8.5.2
.

Let
\mathrm{a}\in\{\operatorname{tr},\operatorname{st}\}.

(1)

\operatorname{Rep}^{\mathrm{a}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}
is an abelian subcategory of
\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{d}
, containing all
M^{\lambda}
and
S^{\lambda}
where
\lambda
is
p^{r}
-stable/truncated, and all
D^{\lambda}
where
\lambda
is
p
-regular,
p^{r}
-stable/truncated.

(2)

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{P}ol^{\mathrm{a}}_{d,p^{r}}\to\left(\operatorname{Rep}^{\mathrm{a}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\right)\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
is a Serre quotient functor whose kernel
\mathcal{K}_{d}^{\mathrm{a}}
has simples given by
L_{\lambda}
where
\lambda^{t}
is
p
-singular,
p^{r}
-stable/truncated.

Recall that we assume
A\subseteq H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
, where
A
is a transitive elementary abelian
p
-group of order
p^{r}
.

Theorem 8.5.3
.

Suppose
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}(H)\to\mathscr{A}
satisfies the following assumptions:

(1)

for all
H
-modules
V
we have
\varphi(V\otimes\operatorname{sgn}_{H})=0
whenever
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(V)=0
, and

(2)

\varphi(\mathbf{1})
is a simple object of
\mathscr{A}
.

Then the OTI functor
\Phi_{\varphi}
(co)restricts to an equivalence
\Phi_{\varphi}:\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\to\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}
, independent of the choice of
\varphi
.

Again, the semisimplification functor
\varphi_{H}:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to(\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H)^{ss}
and the CF functors in Example
5.1.2
satisfy the above assumptions, as well as the conjectural CF functor constructed in
[
10
, Sec. 5.4]
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}:=\varphi(-\otimes\operatorname{sgn})
. Then,
\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}
satisfies the conditions in Theorem
8.4.5
, so
we have a commutative diagram of Serre quotients,

\textstyle{\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d,p^{r}}^{\operatorname{st}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}ol_{d-p^{r},p^{r}}^{\operatorname{tr}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{F}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}.}

It follows that
\Phi_{\varphi}:\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\to\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}
is an equivalence. Since

\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi_{\operatorname{sgn}}}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes\operatorname{sgn}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\varphi}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}\otimes\operatorname{sgn}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes\operatorname{sgn}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d-p^{r}}}

commutes,
\Phi_{\varphi}:\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}\to\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{tr}}_{p^{r}}S_{d}
is also an equivalence.
∎

Corollary 8.5.4
.

Suppose that
V\in\operatorname{Rep}^{\operatorname{st}}_{d,p^{r}}S_{d}
. Then we have a decomposition

V|_{H}=\mathbf{1}^{\oplus\ell}\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{i}N_{i},

where each
N_{i}
is indecomposable and satisfies
\varphi_{\mathbf{a}}(N_{i})=0
for all generic
\mathbf{a}
. In particular,
p\mid\operatorname{dim}(N_{i})
for all
i
.

Proof.

Indeed, this follows by applying Theorem
8.5.3
to the CF functor given by semisimplification with respect to
H
.
∎

9.
Diagrammatics and the proof of Theorem
5.6.1

In order to provide a clear proof of Theorem
5.6.1
, we recall the diagrammatic presentation of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions, see also
[
7
, Section 3.1]
.

9.1.
Trace morphisms for finite groups

We first record a lemma from
[
5
, Prop. 3.6.6]
, on representations of finite groups.
Let
H\subseteq G
be finite groups and
M,N
be
G
-modules. The transfer map

\operatorname{Tr}_{H,G}:\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(M,N)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(M,N)

is defined by

\operatorname{Tr}_{H,G}(f):=\sum\limits_{g\in G/H}gfg^{-1}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(M,N).

Lemma 9.1.1
.

A morphism
h:M\to N
of
G
-modules factors through a module of the form
\operatorname{Ind}_{H}^{G}V
if and only if
h=\operatorname{Tr}_{H,G}(f)
for some
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{H}(M,N)
.

Corollary 9.1.2
.

A
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
satisfies property (2) of Definition
5.1.1
if and only if
\varphi(\operatorname{Tr}_{H^{\prime},H}(f))=0
for all non-transitive subgroups
H^{\prime}\subseteq H
and all
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{H^{\prime}}(M,N)
.

9.2.
Diagrammatics

\mathcal{H}eis_{k}
, where
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. This category is monoidal, with monoidal unit
\mathbf{1}
, and is generated by objects

A degenerate categorical Heisenberg action on
\mathscr{C}
is by definition a
\Bbbk
-linear, monoidal functor
H:\mathcal{H}eis_{k}\to\operatorname{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
. We write
E\coloneqq H(\uparrow)
and
F\coloneqq H(\downarrow)
. The structure maps
\eta,\epsilon,x,T
correspond to the appropriate cups and caps, a dot on a strand, and crossing respectively.

Let us now assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a locally finite abelian category with a degenerate categorical Heisenberg action. We will continue to draw the functors
E,F
as
{\uparrow}
and
{\downarrow}
, and natural transformations between them by the same diagrams used in
\mathcal{H}eis_{k}
.

If
\mathscr{A}
is another locally finite abelian category, then as noted in Section
5.2
,
\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
inherits a natural categorical Heisenberg action, where
{\uparrow}
acts by
E\boxtimes 1
and
{\downarrow}
acts by
F\boxtimes 1
. By abuse of notation, we will use the same diagrammatics for this categorical action as well.

9.3.
Diagrammatics of the OTI functor

Recalling that
E^{n}
admits an action of
S_{n}
, we may define a functor
n

:=

…
n

:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{n}
.

Now let
\varphi:\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}H\to\mathscr{A}
denote a CF functor for
H\subseteq S_{p^{r}}
. We will represent

Lemma 9.3.2
.

The functor
1\boxtimes\varphi:\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\operatorname{Rep}_{\Bbbk}S_{p^{r}}\to\mathscr{C}\boxtimes\mathscr{A}
is a morphism of degenerate categorical Heisenberg actions (see Definition
5.3.1
).

Proof.

It is enough to check the commuting diagrams which correspond to cups, caps, dot and crossing pulling through the dashed strand
1\boxtimes\varphi
. Indeed, the equalities

\displaystyle\hbox to22.9pt{\vbox to27.43pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.62816pt\lower-9.99098pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 0 L 0.39 23.23}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.7071}{0.7071}{-0.7071}{-0.7071}{0.14142pt}{16.92996pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

follow from Lemma
2.2.1
.
We illustrate this by proving the first equality. Let us write

s_{\varphi,E}:(1\boxtimes\varphi)\circ(E\boxtimes 1)\xrightarrow{\sim}(E\boxtimes 1)\circ(1\boxtimes\varphi)

for the 2-isomorphism in
\mathcal{A}b\mathcal{C}at
natural in
E
and
\varphi
. Then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\boxtimes\varphi)(E\boxtimes 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\boxtimes\varphi)(E\boxtimes 1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E\boxtimes 1)(1\boxtimes\varphi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(E\boxtimes 1)(1\boxtimes\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\varphi,E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(1\boxtimes 1_{\varphi})(x\boxtimes 1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\varphi,E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(x\boxtimes 1)(1\boxtimes 1_{\varphi})}

commutes by naturality of
s
in
E
, which shows the first equality.
∎

9.4.
A structural lemma

If
k\geq 0
, then by repeatedly applying the isomorphism (
9.2.3
) we obtain an isomorphism
\iota:E^{n}F\xrightarrow{\sim}FE^{n}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(E^{n-1})^{\oplus k}
, given explicitly by

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}:V\xrightarrow{\sim}FE^{n}(M)

of
S_{n}
-modules.
A straightforward check shows that
W
is also
S_{n}
-stable. Thus

E^{n}F(M)=V\oplus W

is a decomposition of
S_{n}
-modules.

The difficult component of the proof of Theorem
5.6.1
is the following lemma.

Lemma 9.4.2
.

(1)

If
k\geq 0
,
\iota_{M}
induces an isomorphism of
S_{n}
-modules

E^{n}F(M)\cong FE^{n}(M)\oplus\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}(E^{n-1}(M))^{\oplus k},

where
S_{n}
acts on
FE^{n}
and
E^{n}F
via the braiding, and
S_{n-1}
acts on
E^{n-1}
via the braiding.

(2)

Similarly if
k<0
, we have a natural isomorphism of
S_{n}
-modules

FE^{n}(M)\cong E^{n}F(M)\oplus\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}(E^{n-1}(M))^{\oplus(-k)}.

k\geq 0
, with the case of
k<0
being similar. Consider the following subspace of
E^{n}F(M)

The
S_{n-1}
-action on
E^{n}F
, permuting the last
n-1
upward strands, commutes with this matrix. Hence
\iota_{M}:W_{0}\to(E^{n-1}(M))^{\oplus k}
is an isomorphism of
S_{n-1}
-modules, with the action on
E^{n-1}(M)
coming from the braiding. We have now reduced the proof to showing that

\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}W_{0}\to W\xrightarrow{\iota_{M}}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(E^{n-1}(M))^{\oplus k}

is an isomorphism, where the first morphism comes from the universal property of induced modules.
We consider the following coset representatives of
S_{n}/S_{n-1}
which act on the
E
-coloured strands of
E^{n}F
by the crossing diagrams

(9.4.4)

for the leading terms in the latter components of
\iota
. Since the dot is on the far right, it is clear that
c_{\ell,j}\circ t_{\ell}=c_{0,j}
for any
j
.
By writing the components of
\iota
as in Lemma
9.4.1
, we see that the
i=\ell
component of
\iota_{M}\circ t_{\ell}
on
W_{0}
is given by the matrix
(c_{0,0},c_{0,1},\ldots,c_{0,k-1})
. Comparing to (
9.4.3
), we know this is an isomorphism.
Since the
i
th component of
\iota_{M}\circ t_{\ell}(W_{0})
is zero whenever
i>\ell
,
we have indeed shown that the required map is an isomorphism:

\operatorname{Ind}_{S_{n-1}}^{S_{n}}W_{0}=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{n-1}t_{\ell}(W_{0})\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(E^{n-1}(M))^{\oplus k}.

∎

This last result shows that
E^{n}
behaves as desired, up to morphisms annihilated by
\varphi
.
